Dense ReLU Neural Networks for Temporal-Spatial Modeling
Carlos Misael Madrid Padilla, Zhi Zhang, Xiaokai Luo, Daren Wang, Oscar Hernan Madrid Padilla

Abstract
We provide the proofs for all the results in the main paper. Furthermore, additional simulations are presented.



Keywords:

Nonparametric statistics, deep learning, manifold data.

1
Introduction

Neural networks have become a cornerstone in nonparametric statistical methodologies, widely applied in
vision and image classification
(
Krizhevsky et al., 2017
)
, facial recognition
(
Taigman et al., 2014
)
, time series forecasting
(
Graves, 2012
)
, generative models
(
Goodfellow et al., 2014
)
, and advanced architectures like transformers
(
Vaswani et al., 2017
)
and GPT models
(
Radford et al., 2018
)
.

The rise of high-dimensional, complex data has made the curse of dimensionality a key challenge in nonparametric regression
(
Donoho, 2000
)
, though deep neural networks have shown promise in addressing it
(
Bauer and Kohler, 2019
)
. Meanwhile, datasets with temporal and spatial dependencies are increasingly studied in fields like environmental science, epidemiology, and economics
(
Wikle et al., 2019
;
Lawson, 2013
;
Wu et al., 2021
)
.

Despite the demand from applications, most of the statistical theory literature on deep learning assumes that the data are independent (e.g.
McCaffrey and Gallant (1994)
;
Kohler and Krzyżak (2005)
;
Hamers and Kohler (2006)
;
Schmidt-Hieber (2020)
;
Bauer and Kohler (2019)
;
Padilla et al. (2022)
), with the exception of
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
who considered time series data.

In this paper, we concentrate on fully connected deep neural networks utilizing the ReLU activation function due to its applicability
(
Nair and Hinton, 2010
;
Glorot and Bengio, 2010
;
Krizhevsky et al., 2012
)
. We derive non-asymptotic bounds for nonparametric estimation with deep neural networks. Distinct from previous studies, our approach accounts for the dependence of measurements. By integrating these dependencies, our models more faithfully mirror the underlying data structure, thereby improving both predictive performance and theoretical robustness.

1.1
Temporal-Spatial Model

This paper examines a nonparametric regression model that captures temporal and spatial dependencies. Our model assumes that we are given data
\left\{\left(y_{ij},x_{ij}\right)\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
generated as

\displaystyle y_{ij}=f^{*}(x_{ij})+\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij}.

(1)

Here, the index
i
represents time, and
1\leq j\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m_{i}}
indicates the different measurements sampled at time
i
. The observations

\left\{x_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
represent the random locations where the (noisy) temporal-spatial data
\left\{y_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
are observed.
The function
f^{*}:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes the deterministic function of interest, while the stochastic processes
\left\{\gamma_{i}:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
represent the functional spatial noise. Finally,
\left\{\epsilon_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
account for the measurement error.

Without smoothness assumptions on the regression function
f^{*}
, deriving convergence rates for nonparametric regression is infeasible
(
Cover, 1968
)
. For a
(p,C)
-smooth
f^{*}
,

Stone (1982)
established the minimax rate of
n^{-2p/(2p+d)}
, highlighting the curse of dimensionality: higher dimensions
d
dramatically slow convergence. Structural assumptions like additivity, as shown by
Stone (1985)
;
Stone (1994)
, can overcome this limitation.

This paper extends the hierarchical interaction model of
Kohler and Langer (2021)
, encompassing additive and single index models, to include temporal and spatial dependencies, as well as data on manifolds, demonstrating the adaptability of dense neural network estimators to these complex structures.

1.2
Summary of Results

In this paper, we focus on a neural network estimator that minimizes the empirical
\mathcal{L}_{2}
risk over the observed data within the network class
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
, as defined in (
5
), where the network consists of
L
hidden layers and
r
neurons per layer. The estimator is given by:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}=\underset{f\in\mathcal{F}(L,r)}{\arg\min}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\left(y_{ij}-f(x_{ij})\right)^{2}.

(2)

The presence of
m_{i}
measurements for each
i
is reflected in the loss used in (
2
). Notably, the work by
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
addresses only temporal data, without accounting for spatial noise, which is a novel consideration in our approach.

Beyond Independence: Temporal–Spatial Results

Our main result studies this estimator under short-range temporal
dependence in the covariates, measurement errors, and spatial random
effects. Following
Kohler and Langer (2021)
, we assume that the regression
function has a hierarchical composition structure. Such functions are
built by nesting smooth component functions whose smoothness and input
dimensions may vary.

For this class,
Theorem
1

gives a risk bound with two main terms. The first
term is driven by the total number of measurements.
Its rate depends on the smoothness and input dimension of the component
function that is hardest to estimate. The relevant input dimension may
be much smaller than the ambient dimension. In that case, the first
term can mitigate the curse of dimensionality.
When temporal dependence and spatial random effects are absent, its
algebraic order agrees, up to logarithmic factors, with the rate for
fully connected networks in
Kohler and Langer (2021)
.

The second term is driven mainly by the number of
time points. It captures the effects of temporal dependence and the
time-indexed spatial random effects.

For the ordered hierarchical subclass specified in
Lemma
4
, the corresponding terms in the
lower and upper bounds have the same algebraic orders.
They therefore match up to logarithmic factors. This provides a
temporal–spatial counterpart to the no-spatial-noise lower bound in

Schmidt-Hieber (2020)
.

Theorem
1

establishes the upper bound for two explicit fully connected
architectures, one wide and one deep, following

Kohler and Langer (2021)
.
Theorem
2
gives a risk
bound for general estimation families under
approximation and entropy conditions.
These families need not consist of neural networks.
Corollary
3

specializes this result to fully connected ReLU
networks and makes the dependence on depth and width explicit.
This facilitates comparison with the architecture-dependent bounds in

Jiao et al. (2023)
.

The framework also yields two useful special cases. Without temporal
dependence, we obtain a functional-regression result for independent
functional observations. This connects with the classical
functional-regression literature

Cardot et al. (1999)
;
Cardot et al. (2003)
;
Cai and Hall (2006)
;
Hall and Horowitz (2007)
;
Cai and Yuan (2012)
;
Petersen and Müller (2016)
.
Without spatial random effects and with one measurement at each time
point, we obtain a temporal-regression result. The temporal result
extends the independent setting of
Kohler and Langer (2021)
and is related
to the temporally dependent setting of
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
.
Precise assumptions and rates are given in
Section
3
.

Overcoming the Curse of Dimensionality with Deep Neural
Networks and Manifold Learning

This paper also explores the estimation problem for nonparametric
temporal–spatial data when the covariates have low-dimensional
geometric structure. We consider exact manifold support, support near
a manifold, and support on a more general set of low geometric
complexity. Across these settings, the risk bounds retain the
two-term structure described above. The first
term depends on an intrinsic or effective dimension
of the covariate support rather than directly on the ambient dimension,
while the second term is governed mainly by the
number of time points. Precise statements are given in
Section
4
.

When temporal dependence and spatial random effects are absent, the
exact-manifold result recovers the upper-bound rate in

Kohler et al. (2023)
, up to logarithmic factors. The
approximate-manifold and low-Minkowski-dimension results provide
temporal–spatial counterparts to the corresponding i.i.d. results in

Jiao et al. (2023)
.

1.3
Notation

The set of positive integers is denoted by
\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, and the set of natural numbers is denoted by
\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{Z}^{+}\cup\{0\}
. For
n\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, we write
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
For two positive sequences
\{a_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}}
and
\{b_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}}
, we write
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
or
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
if
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
with some constant
C>0
that does not depend on
n
, and
a_{n}=\Theta(b_{n})
or
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if
a_{n}=O(b_{n})
and
b_{n}=O(a_{n})
. Given a
d
-dimensional multi-index
j=\left(j^{(1)},\ldots,j^{(d)}\right)^{T}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}
, we write
\|j\|_{1}=j^{(1)}+\cdots+j^{(d)},j!=j^{(1)}!\cdots j^{(d)}
!,
x^{j}=x_{1}^{j^{(1)}}\cdots x_{d}^{j^{(d)}}
and
\partial^{j}=(\partial^{j^{(1)}}\cdots\partial^{j^{(d)}})/(\partial x_{1}^{j^{(1)}}\cdots\partial x_{d}^{j^{(d)}}).

For a random variable
X
, we denote by
\sigma(X)
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X
, that is, the collection of events determined by
X
. More generally, for a collection
\{X_{t}:t\in I\}
, we write
\sigma(X_{t},\,t\in I)
for the
\sigma
-algebra generated by these random variables. For a
d
-dimensional vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Euclidean and the supremum norms of
x
are denoted by
\|x\|
and
\|x\|_{\infty}
, respectively. The
\ell_{\infty}
norm of a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined by

\|f\|_{\infty}=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x)|.

We denote the supremum norm of
f
restricted to a subset
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\|f\|_{\infty,\mathcal{A}}
. Given a function
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and a probability distribution
\mathbb{P}
over
\mathcal{X}
, the usual
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})
-norm is given by

\|f\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})}:=\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{X}}f^{2}(x)\mathbb{P}(dx)\bigr)^{1/2}=\mathbb{E}\left[f^{2}(X)\right]^{1/2},

where
X
is a random variable with distribution
\mathbb{P}
. The space of functions such that
\|f\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})}<\infty
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})
. Similarly, the
\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})
-inner product is given by

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})}:=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(x)g(x)\mathbb{P}(dx)=\mathbb{E}\left[f(X)g(X)\right].
Given a collection of samples
\left\{x_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
that are identically distributed according to a probability distribution
\mathbb{P}
, define the empirical probability distribution
as
\mathbb{P}_{nm}(x):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\delta_{x_{ij}}(x).

Then the empirical
\mathcal{L}_{2}
-norm is given by

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}\left(\mathbb{P}_{nm}\right)}:=\bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}f^{2}\left(x_{ij}\right)\bigr)^{1/2}=\bigl(\int_{\mathcal{X}}f^{2}(x)\mathbb{P}_{nm}(dx)\bigr)^{1/2}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}\left(\mathbb{P}_{nm}\right)}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}f\left(x_{ij}\right)g\left(x_{ij}\right)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(x)g(x)\mathbb{P}_{nm}(dx),

defines the empirical
\mathcal{L}_{2}
-inner product.
When there is no ambiguity,
\|f\|_{nm}
,
\|f\|_{2}
,
\langle f,g\rangle_{nm}
and
\langle f,g\rangle_{2}
are written as convenient shorthands for
\|f\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{nm})}
,
\|f\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})}
,
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{nm})}
and
\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}(\mathbb{P})}
, respectively. Additionally, if the probability distribution is supported on a subset
\mathcal{A}
, we write
\|f\|_{nm}
as
\|f\|_{nm,\mathcal{A}}
to highlight the dependence on
\mathcal{A}
.
Furthermore, for random variable
x
, if
\mathbb{E}(\exp(tx))\leq\exp(\frac{t^{2}}{2\varpi^{2}})
for any
t>0
, we say
x
is a sub-Gaussian with parameter
\varpi>0
. For a vector
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
a^{(j)}
, for
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, its
j
coordinate.

1.4
Outline

The paper is organized as follows: Section
2
provides background on nonparametric regression and neural network approximation. Section
3
explores the convergence rates of neural network estimators under different dependence structures. Section
4
extends these rates to input data on lower-dimensional manifolds. Section
5
presents numerical experiments on simulated and real-world data. Section
6
concludes with future research directions, with full proofs in the Supplementary Material.

2
Overview of Fully Connected Neural Networks

In this section, we provide a comprehensive overview of the architecture of fully connected neural networks. The architecture of a neural network, denoted by
(L,k)
, is characterized by two primary components: the number of hidden layers
L
, which is a positive integer, and the width vector
k=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{L})\in\mathbf{N}^{L}
, which specifies the number of neurons in each of the
L
hidden layers.

A multilayer feedforward neural network with architecture
(L,k)
, employing the ReLU activation function
\rho(x)=\max(0,x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, can be mathematically represented as a real-valued function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{L}}c_{1,i}^{(L)}f_{i}^{(L)}(x)+c_{1,0}^{(L)}

(3)

for some weights
c_{1,0}^{(L)},\ldots,c_{1,k_{L}}^{(L)}\in\mathbb{R}
and for
f_{i}^{(L)}
’s recursively defined by

\displaystyle f_{i}^{(s)}(x)=\rho\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k_{s-1}}c_{i,j}^{(s-1)}f_{j}^{(s-1)}(x)+c_{i,0}^{(s-1)}\right)

(4)

for some
c_{i,0}^{(s-1)},\dots,c_{i,k_{s-1}}^{(s-1)}\in\mathbb{R}
,

s\in\{2,\dots,L\}
,
and
f_{i}^{(1)}(x)=\rho\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}c_{i,j}^{(0)}x^{(j)}+c_{i,0}^{(0)}\right)

for some
c_{i,0}^{(0)},\dots,c_{i,d}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
.

For simplification, we assume that all hidden layers possess an identical number of neurons. Consequently, we define the space
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
, as per
Kohler and Langer (2021)
, to represent the set of neural networks with
L
hidden layers and
r
neurons per layer:

Definition 1
.

The space of neural networks with
L
hidden layers and
r
neurons per layer is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(L,r)=\bigl\{f:\;f\text{ is of the form \eqref{eq:form of approximation function 0}}\;\text{and \eqref{eq:form of approximation function L} with }k_{1}=k_{2}=\ldots=k_{L}=r\bigr\}.

(5)

Here, the neurons in the neural network can be viewed as a computational unit. The input of any neuron
i
in the
s-
th layer is associated with the output of all
k_{s-1}
units
j
in the
(s-1)-
th layer with weights
c_{i,j}^{(s-1)}
through the ReLU function
\rho(\cdot)
. Such a kind of recursive construction of a fully connected neural network can be represented as an acyclic graph.

One key feature of our neural network architecture is that no network sparsity assumption is needed. The network class
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
represents a fully connected feedforward neural network, where each neuron is connected to every neuron in the previous layer.

To avoid the so–called curse of dimensionality, we consider
f^{*}
be in the space of hierarchical composition models
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
for some
l\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq[1,\infty)\times\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, which is described next.

Definition 2
.

Let
p=q+s
for some
q\in\mathbf{N}=\mathbf{Z}^{+}\cup\left\{0\right\}
and
0<s\leq 1
. A function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is called
(p,C)
-smooth, if for every
\alpha=\left(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}\right)\in\mathbf{N}^{d}
, where
d\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, with
\|\alpha\|_{1}=q
, the partial derivative
\partial^{q}g/\left(\partial z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\partial z_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}\right)
exists and satisfies

\left|\frac{\partial^{q}g}{\partial z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\partial z_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}}\left(z\right)-\frac{\partial^{q}g}{\partial z_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\ldots\partial z_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}}\left(w\right)\right|\leq C\|z-w\|^{s}

for all
z,w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Euclidean norm.

Definition 3

(Space of Hierarchical Composition Models,
Kohler and Langer (2021)
)
.

Let
l\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
and the order and smoothness constraint
\mathcal{P}\subseteq[1,\infty)\times\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, the space of hierarchical composition models is defined recursively. For
l=1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(1,\mathcal{P}):={}\bigl\{h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\quad h(z)=u\left(z^{(\pi(1))},\ldots,z^{(\pi(K))}\right),

\displaystyle\text{where }u:\mathbb{R}^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\text{ is}\;(p,C)\text{-smooth for some }(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle\text{and }\pi:\{1,\ldots,K\}\rightarrow\{1,\ldots,d\}\bigr\}.

For
l>1
, we recursively define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P}):={}\bigl\{h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}:\quad h(z)=u\left(f_{1}(z),\ldots,f_{K}(z)\right),

\displaystyle\text{where }u:\mathbb{R}^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\text{ is}\;(p,C)\text{-smooth for some }(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}\quad\text{and }f_{i}\in\mathcal{H}(l-1,\mathcal{P})\bigr\}.

The pair
(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}
may vary across component functions in
the recursive representation. Thus,
\mathcal{P}
records the smoothness
levels and input dimensions that may occur throughout the hierarchy.
Notably, additive models, single index models, and projection pursuit
can be viewed as special cases of the hierarchical composition model.

Here, the function class
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
describes the relationships between the input and output of the network, where
(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}
describes the smoothness and order constraint of the hierarchical composition model. Notably, additive models, single index models, and projection pursuit can be viewed as special cases of the hierarchical composition model.

3
ReLU Dense NN for Temporal-Spatial Models

3.1
Main Result

In this section, we establish the main risk bound for the dense ReLU neural network estimator under temporal-spatial dependence. We begin by recalling the notion of
\beta
-mixing, which we use to quantify temporal dependence; see
Doukhan (2012)
for a comprehensive review.

Using the notation introduced in Section
1.3
we define
\beta
-mixing as follows. For two
\sigma
-fields
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, define

\beta(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{C\in\mathcal{B}}\big|\mathbb{P}(C)-\mathbb{P}(C\mid\mathcal{A})\big|\Big].

A sequence of
\sigma
-fields
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}}
is said to be
\beta
-mixing if

\displaystyle\beta_{s}=\sup_{t\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}}\beta\!\left(\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{\ell},\ \ell\leq t),\,\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{\ell},\ \ell\geq t+s)\right)\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }s\to\infty.

Accordingly, a sequence of random variables
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}}
is called
\beta
-mixing if the associated sequence of
\sigma
-fields
\{\sigma(X_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}}
is
\beta
-mixing. If the data are independent, then the corresponding
\beta
-mixing coefficients are zero.

We now state the assumptions under which the main theorem is established. They are grouped into four categories: the observation model, the design and dependence structure, the noise conditions, and the regularity assumptions on the regression function and sampling scheme.

Assumption 1

(Observation model)
.

We consider the temporal-spatial regression model introduced in (
1
), namely

\displaystyle y_{ij}=f^{*}(x_{ij})+\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij},

(6)

for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
, where
x_{ij}\in[0,1]^{d}
,
f^{*}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma_{i}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\epsilon_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption
1
states the temporal-spatial regression model for clarity and establishes notation used throughout the analysis. The response is decomposed into a deterministic signal
f^{*}
, a time-indexed random spatial effect
\gamma_{i}
, and a measurement error term
\epsilon_{ij}
. The component
\gamma_{i}
captures variation in the underlying spatial process across the domain where the data are collected. This variation may arise from natural randomness or application-specific variability and may still be present even in the absence of measurement error. The term
\epsilon_{ij}
represents additional noise due to imperfect observations, such as measurement devices or external interference. This separation distinguishes variability in the underlying signal from variability due to measurement error and arises naturally in applications with repeated spatial observations over time.

We next formalize the assumptions on these noise components.

Assumption 2

(Noise conditions)
.

a.
[sub-Gaussian property of
\epsilon
] For a fixed
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, let
j\in\{1,\ldots,m_{i}\}
. We assume

\mathbb{E}(\epsilon_{ij}\mid x_{ij})=0,

and for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
\sigma_{\epsilon}>0
such that for all
i
and
j
, almost surely,

\mathbb{E}\{\exp(t\epsilon_{ij})\mid x_{ij}\}\leq\exp\biggl(\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}t^{2}}{2}\biggr).

b.
[Marginal spatial distribution]
We assume that the spatial noise processes
\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
are identically distributed with mean zero, i.e.,

{\mathbb{E}}[\gamma_{i}(x)]=0\quad\text{for all }x\in[0,1]^{d}.

Moreover, defining

\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}:=\sup_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}{\mathbb{E}}[\gamma_{i}(x)^{2}],

we assume that
\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}<\infty
and that there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\sup_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\exp\{c\gamma_{i}^{2}(x)\}\bigr)<\infty.

c.
[Moment condition] There exist
r,q>1
such that
\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{q}=1
, and for all
i\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
,

\sup_{i\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}}\,\mathbb{E}\left(\|\gamma_{i}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2q}\right)<\infty.

See Lemma 21 in the Supplementary Material.

Assumption
2
controls the stochastic behavior of the noise components. Part
a
imposes a standard sub-Gaussian condition on the measurement errors. The exponential moment condition in part
b
ensures that the spatial noise has uniformly controlled tails across the domain. Part
c
imposes a moment condition on the spatial noise
\gamma_{i}
. This condition is standard in the analysis of stochastic processes and is used to control higher-order stochastic terms in the analysis, for instance when applying Hölder-type inequalities and bounding
\mathcal{L}_{2}
-norm fluctuations. These conditions are satisfied in many common settings, including Gaussian processes, bounded stochastic processes, processes with sub-Gaussian tails, as well as random basis expansion and Karhunen–Loève models. See Supplementary Material S10 for concrete examples.

We now turn to the remaining components of the model, namely the underlying regression function and the sampling scheme.

Assumption 3

(Regularity of
f^{*}
and sampling balance)
.

a.
[Same order condition of
m_{i}
] There exist positive constants
c
and
C
such that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we have

cm\leq m_{i}\leq Cm,

where
m
is the harmonic mean of
m_{i}
defined by

\displaystyle m=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\right)^{-1}.

(7)

b.
[
\ell_{\infty}
-bound of regression function] Suppose that

\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{4},

where
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
is some parameter depending on
n
and
m
, with
m
as before. We allow
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
to grow to infinity as
n
or
m
increase.

c.
[Hierarchical composition structure]
Let
f^{*}
be in the class
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
as in
Definition
3
. By
Definition
2
,
each component function
g
in the recursive representation of
f^{*}

is associated with a pair
(p_{g},K_{g})\in\mathcal{P}
, where

p_{g}=q_{g}+s_{g},\qquad q_{g}\in\mathbf{N},\qquad s_{g}\in(0,1].

Let
K_{\max}
be the largest input dimension and
p_{\max}
the
largest smoothness among the component functions. In addition to these defining conditions, impose the following uniform
regularity requirements. For every component function
g
, all partial
derivatives of order at most
q_{g}
are uniformly bounded by

C_{\mathrm{Smooth}}
. Moreover, each
g
is also Lipschitz continuous with
Lipschitz constant
C_{\mathrm{Lip}}\geq 1
. Also, assume that

\max\{p_{\max},K_{\max}\}=O(1)
. Let

\phi_{nm}=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+K}}.

Assumption
3
controls both the complexity of the regression function and the balance of the sampling scheme across time. The condition in
a
requires that the number of observations per time index is of the same order. This is standard in the literature with spatial or functional noise. For instance,
Wang et al. (2022)
studies nonparametric covariance estimation for functional data and imposes a similar assumption; see Page 3 of that paper. The bound in
b
imposes a uniform
\ell_{\infty}
constraint on the regression function. This supports the truncation step and ensures stability of the estimator in the presence of noise. Allowing the bound
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
to grow with the sample size provides flexibility while still controlling large deviations. Finally, the structural assumption in
c
places
f^{*}
in a hierarchical composition class. This captures low-dimensional structure within high-dimensional inputs and enables neural networks to adapt to compositional features of the regression function; see
Kohler and Langer (2021)
;
Kohler et al. (2023)
.

In addition, part

c
imposes uniform derivative and Lipschitz bounds on the component
functions and requires their maximal smoothness and input dimension to
remain bounded. These additional conditions are used to obtain the
neural-network approximation bound in Supplementary Lemma 1;
see also
Kohler and Langer (2021)
;
Kohler et al. (2023)
.

Having specified the conditions on each component of the model, we now turn to the sampling design and temporal dependence structure.

Assumption 4

(Design and dependence structure)
.

a.
[Support of
x
] The design points
\{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
are supported on
[0,1]^{d}
.

b.
[
\beta
-mixing time series] Let

\begin{gathered}\sigma_{x}(i)=\sigma(\{x_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}),\qquad\sigma_{\gamma}(i)=\sigma(\gamma_{i}),\qquad\sigma_{\epsilon}(i)=\sigma(\{\epsilon_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}).\end{gathered}

Suppose that
\{\sigma_{x}(i)\}_{i=0}^{n}
,
\{\sigma_{\gamma}(i)\}_{i=0}^{n}
, and
\{\sigma_{\epsilon}(i)\}_{i=0}^{n}
are
\beta
-mixing with
\beta
coefficients
\beta(s)
. See Supplementary Material
S3-B1
for the
\beta
-mixing definition. The
\beta
coefficients are assumed to have exponential decay,

\beta(s)\lesssim e^{-C_{\beta}s}

for some constant
C_{\beta}>0
.

c.
[Independence between
x
,
\gamma
, and
\epsilon
] For
(i,j)\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\times\{1,\ldots,m_{i}\}
, the
\sigma
-fields

\begin{gathered}\sigma(x_{ij}),\qquad\sigma_{\gamma}=\sigma(\{\gamma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}),\qquad\sigma_{\epsilon}^{(ij)}=\sigma\!\left(\{\epsilon_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\}_{i^{\prime}=1,j^{\prime}=1}^{n,m_{i}}\backslash\{\epsilon_{ij}\}\right)\end{gathered}

are mutually independent. In addition, for each fixed
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we assume that
\{x_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{i}}
are independent and identically distributed.

Assumption
4
specifies how dependence is incorporated into the model. It fixes the support of the design in Part
a
. Then, in Part
b
we allow for weak temporal dependence through
\beta
-mixing with exponentially decaying coefficients. This captures short-range dependence across time, which is commonly observed in spatio-temporal data.
Our framework allows for dependence in the design, the spatial noise, and the measurement errors. In contrast, prior work such as
Kohler and Langer (2021)
;
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
;
Kohler et al. (2023)
typically assumes independence and does not account for spatial noise. As a result, the concentration analysis remains valid under structured temporal-spatial dependence.

Part
c
imposes a standard exogeneity condition, meaning that the design variables are independent of the noise components. Specifically, the covariates are assumed to be independent of the spatial noise and of measurement errors across different indices, as is common in the literature; see, among others,
Schmidt-Hieber (2020)
;
Kohler and Langer (2021)
;
Wang et al. (2022)
. At the same time, the formulation allows for localized dependence at the observation level. This is because independence is only required with respect to
\sigma_{\epsilon}^{(ij)}
. In particular, this permits dependence between
x_{ij}
and its corresponding measurement error
\epsilon_{ij}
, while preserving independence across different indices. Such localized dependence is related to the notion of endogeneity, which arises naturally in many econometric applications, where covariates may be correlated with unobserved disturbances; see, e.g.,
Wooldridge (2010)
. In our setting, this effect is restricted to the observation level. This maintains tractability while still allowing for additional modeling flexibility.

With these assumptions in place, we can now state the main result.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
–
4
hold and let
\widehat{f}
be the estimator in
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
defined in (
2
), with its number of layers
L
and number of neurons per layer
r
set as either one of the following two cases:

\displaystyle\text{{Case 1.} a wide network with}\quad L\asymp\log(nm)\quad\text{and}\quad r\asymp\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}},

(8)

\displaystyle\text{{Case 2.} a deep network with}\quad L\asymp\log(nm)\cdot\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}}\quad\text{and}\quad r=r_{0},

where
r_{0}\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
is a sufficiently large constant independent of
n
and
m
.
Let

\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\left\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\right\}\cdot\log^{1/2}(nm).

and
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
.
Then with probability approaching one, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim a_{n}\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\right)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{1}{nm}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\log^{7.3}(nm)

(9)

\displaystyle+a_{n}\frac{\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\right)\log(nm)}{n}.

The first term in (
9
) is the main approximation–estimation term. It is driven by the total number of observations, of order
nm
, together with the compositional complexity of
f^{*}
, encoded through the pairs
(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}
. In the independent case, as established in Corollary
5
, this term is of order

\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+K}}

up to logarithmic factors.
In the dependent setting, temporal dependence enters through the blocking and coupling argument for the
\beta
-mixing sequence, applied to the full time-indexed process (including the covariates, measurement errors, and spatial random effects). At the level of the proof, this leads to an intermediate complexity term of order

\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{S}{nm}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}},

where
S
denotes the block length. Under exponential
\beta
-mixing, choosing
S\asymp\log n
affects this contribution only through an additional logarithmic factor, so that the algebraic rate is preserved up to logarithmic corrections. Consequently, the first term matches the standard neural-network approximation rate associated with hierarchical function classes, up to logarithmic factors, see Theorem 3 of Kohler and Langer
(
2021
)
.

We now clarify how the logarithmic factors arising at different stages of the proof combine to produce the factor
\log^{7.3}(nm)
. The first contribution comes from the metric entropy bound for the truncated version of the neural network class
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
. This bound controls the complexity of the network class and contributes a factor of order
\log^{4.3}(nm)
. It follows from the VC-dimension bound of
Bartlett et al. (2019)
, together with the empirical covering-number argument of
Györfi et al. (2002)
; see also Lemma 19 of
Kohler and Langer (2021)
and Supplementary Lemma 24 in Section S8. The next contribution arises from the localization and symmetrization arguments used to compare the empirical and population norms. These arguments contribute an additional factor of order
\log(nm)
, raising the logarithmic exponent from
4.3
to
5.3
and leading to the intermediate term
a_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}+1)\phi_{nm/S}\log^{5.3}(nm)
. Another logarithmic factor comes from the truncation level, since
\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}+1\lesssim(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1)\log(nm)
. Finally, temporal dependence is handled through a blocking and coupling argument with block size
S\asymp\log n
. Because
\phi_{nm/S}\leq S\phi_{nm}
, this step contributes at most one further logarithmic factor. Combining these contributions gives the displayed
\log^{7.3}(nm)
term. Thus, the exponent
7.3
reflects the cumulative effect of the entropy, localization, truncation, and blocking bounds used in our proof and is not claimed to be optimal.

Related work shows that the exponent of the logarithmic factor can be reduced in other settings. In particular, Ou and Bölcskei
(
2026
)
develop refined covering-number bounds for deep ReLU networks, including networks with unrestricted weights and truncated outputs. In a related i.i.d. Lipschitz-regression setting, they combine these bounds with bounded-weight approximation to remove a
\log^{6}n
factor. Obtaining a corresponding improvement for our hierarchical
\beta
-mixing temporal–spatial model would require a separate analysis of the approximation, empirical-process, norm-comparison, and spatial-random-effect arguments, which is beyond the scope of the present paper.

Theorem
1
treats the
two canonical wide and deep architectures used in
Kohler and Langer (2021)
.
Its proof depends on the architecture only through a uniform
approximation bound for
f^{*}
and empirical covering-number bounds
for the corresponding truncated network class.
Replacing these architecture-specific inputs by abstract approximation
and entropy conditions yields a risk bound for

general estimation families, not only neural networks
.

Theorem
2

states this bound. We present the explicit rate first to emphasize the
main statistical conclusion.

To state the result, we use the block notation introduced in Supplementary Material
S11
. For a block length
S_{n}
and
s\in\{0,\ldots,S_{n}-1\}
, let
\mathcal{J}_{e,s}
and
\mathcal{J}_{o,s}
denote the even and odd block index sets. The associated empirical norms are denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}
and
\|\cdot\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}
.

Theorem 2

(General
function-class
bound)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
–
4
hold
, except for Assumption
3
c
. For each
n
and
m
, let
\mathfrak{F}_{nm}
be a nonempty deterministic pointwise measurable class of measurable functions from
[0,1]^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\widehat{f}
be
a measurable minimizer
over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm}}

of the empirical criterion in
(
2
). Assume that there exists
a deterministic

\overline{f}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm}}
such that

\|f^{*}-\overline{f}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq\varepsilon_{nm}^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\qquad\|\overline{f}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathcal{A}_{nm}}

for some sequence
\varepsilon_{nm}>0
.
Let
(S_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence satisfying

C_{\beta}\log(n)\leq S_{n}\leq n,

for a sufficiently large constant
C_{\beta}>0
depending only on the mixing rate in Assumption
4
.
Suppose further that there exist positive sequences
(\rho_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\widetilde{\rho}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such that, for all sufficiently large
n
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\;\max_{0\leq s\leq S_{n}-1}\sup_{z_{1},\dots,z_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\in[0,1]^{d}}\frac{\log\mathcal{N}\!\left(\delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm,\mathcal{A}_{nm}}},\|\cdot\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\right)}{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m\,\log(\delta^{-1})}\leq C\,\rho_{n},\end{aligned}

(10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\;\max_{0\leq s\leq S_{n}-1}\sup_{z_{1},\dots,z_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\in[0,1]^{d}}\frac{\log\mathcal{N}\!\left(\delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm,\mathcal{A}_{nm}}},\|\cdot\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\right)}{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m\,\log(\delta^{-1})}\leq C\,\rho_{n},\end{aligned}

(11)

and

\displaystyle\sup_{0<\delta\leq 1}\;\sup_{z_{1},\dots,z_{nm}\in[0,1]^{d}}\frac{\log\mathcal{N}\!\left(\delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm,\mathcal{A}_{nm}}},\|\cdot\|_{nm}\right)}{nm\,\log(\delta^{-1})}

\displaystyle\leq C\,\widetilde{\rho}_{n},

(12)

where
C>0
is a universal constant and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm,\mathcal{A}_{nm}}}:=\{f_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}:f\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm}}\}.

Let

\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\}\sqrt{\log(nm)},

and let
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
. Then, with probability approaching one,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{nm}^{2}+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\,\rho_{n}\,\log(nm)

(13)

\displaystyle+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\,\widetilde{\rho}_{n}\,\log(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S_{n})+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}.

Theorem
2
decomposes the
risk bound into an approximation term, two entropy-driven stochastic
terms, and contributions arising from temporal dependence and spatial
noise. The quantity
\varepsilon_{nm}^{2}
measures how well
f^{*}
can be
approximated by the chosen family
\mathfrak{F}_{nm}
.
The hierarchical composition assumption is not
required for this result. However, when

\mathfrak{F}_{nm}=\mathcal{F}(L_{nm},r_{nm})
and
f^{*}
belongs to the
hierarchical composition class
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
,
the approximation term reflects the compositional structure of
f^{*}
.
In particular, the hierarchy level
l
is naturally associated with the
depth used to approximate the recursive composition,
while the local pairs
(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}
determine the approximation
difficulty of the component functions.

The stochastic terms are determined by the covering number complexity of the
truncated class

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathfrak{F}_{nm,\mathcal{A}_{nm}}}
.
The blockwise entropy rate
\rho_{n}
, defined through
(
10
)–(
11
),
controls the empirical processes arising from the blocking argument.
The global entropy rate
\widetilde{\rho}_{n}
, defined through
(
12
), controls
the corresponding full-sample empirical process.

The remaining terms have distinct sources. The term

a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S_{n})
is the coupling error arising from
temporal dependence. The term

\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}/n
captures the contribution of the time-indexed
spatial random effects.

We now specialize
Theorem
2
to the fully
connected ReLU class
\mathcal{F}(L_{nm},r_{nm})
in
Definition
1
.
Lemma 24 in the Supplementary material allows the entropy rates

\rho_{n}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{n}
to be expressed directly in terms of the
network depth and width.
The depth and width may follow deterministic
sequences other than those in (
8
).
This gives the following architecture-dependent bound.

Corollary 3

(Architecture-dependent risk bound)
.

Retain the hypotheses and notation of Theorem
2
, except for the entropy conditions (
10
)–(
12
).

For each
n
and
m
, let

L_{nm},r_{nm}\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
be deterministic and set

\mathfrak{F}_{nm}=\mathcal{F}(L_{nm},r_{nm})
,
to be the network functions class defined in Equation (
1
).

Set
\mathfrak{V}_{nm}:=L_{nm}^{2}r_{nm}^{2}\log\!\left(eL_{nm}r_{nm}^{2}\right)
. Then
the entropy conditions
(
10
)–(
12
)
hold with
\rho_{n}\lesssim S_{n}\mathfrak{V}_{nm}(nm)^{-1}\log^{1.3}(nm)
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{n}\lesssim\mathfrak{V}_{nm}(nm)^{-1}\log^{1.3}(nm)
. Consequently, with probability approaching one,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\lesssim\;\varepsilon_{nm}^{2}+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\frac{S_{n}\mathfrak{V}_{nm}}{nm}\log^{2.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S_{n})+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}.

(14)

The proof is given in Supplementary Material S11.
The corollary keeps the approximation error explicit.
Different depth–width choices can therefore be assessed through

\varepsilon_{nm}^{2}
and
\mathfrak{V}_{nm}
.

To recover
Theorem
1
, impose
Assumption
3
c
and choose either
architecture in (
8
).
Coinsider the term
\phi_{N}:=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}N^{-2p/(2p+K)}
.
Lemma 1 in the Supplementary material gives

\varepsilon_{nm}^{2}\lesssim\phi_{nm}
, and both architecture regimes
satisfy
\mathfrak{V}_{nm}\lesssim nm\,\phi_{nm}\log^{3}(nm)
.
Assumption
3
b
, together with

\varepsilon_{nm}\to 0
, ensures that

\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{f}}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathcal{A}_{nm}
for all
sufficiently large
n
.

Under exponential
\beta
-mixing,
choose
S_{n}\asymp\log n
with a sufficiently large
proportionality constant. Then

\beta(S_{n})\lesssim n^{-1}
.
Together with

\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\lesssim(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2})\log(nm)
,
these relations reduce
Corollary
3
to
(
9
).
Hence both canonical architecture regimes are covered by the corollary.

A complementary line of work is
Jiao et al. (2023)
. It studies nonasymptotic error bounds for deep neural network estimators in the classical i.i.d. regression model

Y=f_{0}(X)+\eta,

with independent observations and sub-exponential noise. In terms of our notation, their regression function
f_{0}
corresponds to
f^{*}
, their covariate
X
corresponds to
x_{ij}
, and their response
Y
corresponds to
y_{ij}
in the special case of (
1
) with
\gamma_{i}=0
and
m_{i}=1
.

There are several important differences. First, their analysis assumes independent data, whereas our framework allows for temporal dependence through a
\beta
-mixing structure acting on the full time-indexed process, including covariates, measurement errors, and spatial random effects. Second,
Jiao et al. (2023)
assumes that
f_{0}
is Hölder smooth and exploits structural assumptions on the distribution of the covariates, such as approximate manifold support, exact manifold support, or low Minkowski dimension. By contrast, our framework captures complexity through the compositional structure of the regression function via
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
. Third, their results are formulated in terms of explicit architectural quantities for the feedforward network class
F_{D,W,U,S,B}
, where
D
denotes depth,
W
width,
U
the number of hidden-layer neurons,
S
the number of network parameters, and
B
a uniform output bound. Theorem
2
instead
separates the approximation and entropy inputs required by the
temporal–spatial analysis
for a general estimation family
\mathfrak{F}_{nm}
,
which need not consist of neural networks.
Corollary
3

specializes this result to fully connected ReLU
networks and expresses the entropy contribution directly through

L_{nm}
and
r_{nm}
.

Despite these differences, both approaches decompose the risk into approximation and stochastic complexity terms. Under the correspondence above, the result of
Jiao et al. (2023)
can be stated directly in our notation. Specializing their network class to an equal-width fully connected network replaces their depth and width by
L_{nm}
and
r_{nm}
, respectively. Since
m_{i}=1
in this comparison,
nm=n
. For a fixed input dimension and a fixed uniform output bound, Lemma 3.2 of
Jiao et al. (2023)
bounds the expected square
\mathcal{L}_{2}
error by the network approximation error plus a constant multiple of

L_{nm}^{2}r_{nm}^{2}\log(L_{nm}r_{nm}^{2})(nm)^{-1}\log^{5}(nm)
.
The corresponding stochastic term in Corollary
3
is

a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}S_{n}L_{nm}^{2}r_{nm}^{2}\log\!\left(eL_{nm}r_{nm}^{2}\right)(nm)^{-1}\log^{2.3}(nm)
,
where
S_{n}
is the block length used to control temporal dependence. Thus, up to logarithmic and dependence-related factors, the two
stochastic bounds have the same algebraic dependence on the network
depth and width.

A further distinction is that temporal dependence in our framework is handled through a blocking and coupling argument. This introduces the additional dependence term

a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S_{n}),

and requires controlling the empirical process under
\beta
-mixing rather than independence.
In addition, the presence of time-indexed spatial random effects contributes the term
\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}/n
, reflecting variability at the temporal scale. Thus, while the algebraic approximation–estimation trade-off remains analogous, our result extends architectural risk bounds for deep neural network estimators to temporally dependent data with spatial structure.

Remark 1
.

Suppose that each level
i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
of the hierarchical
composition model has smoothness
p_{i}
and dimension
K_{i}
, with

K_{l}\leq K_{l-1}\leq\cdots\leq K_{1}\leq d
. In the absence of spatial
noise, the leading term in the rate of
Theorem
1
is

(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+1)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{1}{nm}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\log^{7.3}(nm).

Up to a logarithmic factor, this term matches the
{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
lower bound
in Theorem 3 of
Schmidt-Hieber (2020)
; see also
Supplementary Lemma 23.

In the presence of spatial noise, the rate for the same special case becomes

\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{-2p}{2p+K}}+n^{-1},

ignoring logarithmic factors. A natural question arises: is this rate optimal for this special case? A partial answer is provided in the following lemma, which extends Theorem 3 in
Schmidt-Hieber (2020)
to the temporal-spatial setting.

Lemma 4
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
4
hold. Recall that
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
denotes the hierarchical composition model for the function class.

Consider the case

\mathcal{P}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}:=\left\{\left(p_{1},K_{1}\right),\left(p_{2},K_{2}\right),\ldots,\left(p_{l},K_{l}\right)\right\},

where
p_{i}\in[1,\infty)
and
K_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
for every

i\in\{1,\ldots,l\}
, with

K_{l}\leq K_{l-1}\leq\cdots\leq K_{1}\leq d
.

Let
\widetilde{f}
be any estimator based on the observations
\{(x_{ij},y_{ij})\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim\sup}\,\underset{f^{*}\in\mathcal{H}(l,\widetilde{\mathcal{P}})}{\sup}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\|\widetilde{f}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\geq C_{\text{opt}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}+\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{1}{nm}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\Bigr)\Bigr)>0,

where
C_{\text{opt}}>0
is a constant.

Specializing
Theorem
1

to the setting of Lemma
4
yields an upper bound that
matches the lower bound in that lemma up to logarithmic factors.
Hence the rate displayed above is minimax optimal up to
logarithmic factors in this particular setting.
Lemma
4
thereby extends the lower-bound result
of Theorem 3 in
Schmidt-Hieber (2020)
from the model
without spatial noise to the temporal–spatial setting. In this
extension, the additional
n^{-1}
term accounts for the
time-indexed spatial random effects.

Finally, it is worth considering the implications of relaxing
Assumption 4
b
. Suppose instead that the mixing coefficients decay
polynomially, so that
\beta(s)\lesssim s^{-\alpha}
for some

\alpha>0
. In this case, we obtain the rate

\displaystyle a_{n}\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1}{n^{\alpha}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+\frac{(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2})\log(nm)}{n}+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n},

provided that

m\geq\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}n^{\alpha\left(\frac{2p+K}{2p}\right)}.

The difference from the exponential-decay setting arises in the
blocking argument. Under exponential decay, choosing

S\asymp\log n
reduces the coupling error
\beta(S)
to order at most

n^{-1}
, while the effective blockwise sample size
nm/S
differs
from
nm
only by a logarithmic factor. Under polynomial decay, the
same choice gives only

\beta(S)\lesssim(\log n)^{-\alpha}
, so longer blocks are required.
Taking
S=n
makes the coupling error of order at most
n^{-\alpha}

and reduces the effective blockwise sample size to
m
. The condition
above ensures that

\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}m^{-2p/(2p+K)}\leq n^{-\alpha}
.
Consequently, the blockwise estimation and coupling terms are both of
order at most
n^{-\alpha}
, up to logarithmic and noise factors. The
remaining
n^{-1}
-scale terms are unaffected by the change in the
mixing rate. See Supplementary Corollary 3 for
details.

3.2
Functional Regression with Independent Observations

In the case that there is no temporal dependence, our model in (
1
) reduces to the usual functional regression model. This includes as a particular case the linear functional regression setting studied in
Cardot et al. (1999)
;
Cardot et al. (2003)
;
Cai and Hall (2006)
;
Hall and Horowitz (2007)
;
Cai and Yuan (2012)
;
Petersen and Müller (2016)
.

We now give the estimation error of estimator under functional regression model with no temporal dependence.

Corollary 5
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
–
4
hold except Assumption
4
b
.
Consider the model
y_{ij}=f^{*}(x_{ij})+\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij},
where
\{x_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
are i.i.d.
d
-dimensional random variables supported on
[0,1]^{d}
,
\{\epsilon_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
are i.i.d sub-Gaussian random variables with sub-Gaussian parameter
\sigma_{\epsilon}
,
\{\gamma_{i}:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\}_{i=1}^{n}
is a collection of i.i.d. separable stochastic processes.
Then with
L
,
r
and
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
as in
Theorem

1
,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\lesssim a_{n}\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\right)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{1}{nm}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n},

(15)

holds with probability approaching to one. Here
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
.

Thus, compared to the original temporal-spatial model upper bound in Theorem
1
, the rate for functional regression with independent observations does not include the term
which is due to the temporal dependence.

3.3
Model with Temporal Dependence without Spatial Dependence

When
\gamma_{i}(\cdot)=0
and
m_{i}=1
for all
i
, we simplify notation by writing
x_{i}
instead of
x_{i1}
,
y_{i}
instead of
y_{i1}
, and
\epsilon_{i}
instead of
\epsilon_{i1}
. In this case, the model in (
1
) reduces to a time series regression model without spatial dependence,

y_{i}=f^{*}(x_{i})+\epsilon_{i}.

In this setting, the observations are indexed only by time, and dependence arises through the temporal structure of the sequence
\{x_{i},\epsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

This formulation is closely related to the framework studied in
Kohler and Langer (2021)
, where independence is assumed. Here, we relax this assumption and allow for temporal dependence through a
\beta
-mixing condition, while maintaining the same estimator defined in (
2
). Compared to the full model in (
1
), the absence of the spatial component
\gamma_{i}
eliminates the additional variability associated with time-indexed random effects, and naturally leads to the case
m_{i}=1
.

We now establish the corresponding risk bound under temporal dependence.

Corollary 6
.

Suppose that Assumptions
1
–
4
hold with
m_{i}=1
and
\gamma_{i}=0
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then the model can be written as
y_{i}=f^{*}(x_{i})+\epsilon_{i}.
Let
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
.
Moreover, with the
L
and
r
chosen as
Theorem

1
,
and
\mathcal{A}_{n}\asymp\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot\log^{1/2}(n)
, the estimator (
2
)
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}-f^{*}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle a_{n}(\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}+1)\log^{7.3}(n)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}},

with probability approaching to one.

Compared to the rate in
Theorem

1
, the upper bound in Corollary
6
does not involve
\sigma_{\gamma}
, reflecting the absence of spatial random effects. As a result, the additional
n^{-1}
-scale contribution present in the temporal-spatial model disappears, and the rate is driven entirely by the approximation–estimation term at the scale
n
.

This setting is also closely related to
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
, which studies deep neural network estimators for temporally dependent data. In their framework, the data are generated according to

Y_{t}=f_{0}(X_{t})+\epsilon_{t},

where
\{X_{t}\}
is a strictly stationary and exponentially
\alpha
-mixing process, while the noise sequence
\{\epsilon_{t}\}
satisfies a Bernstein-type moment condition and is independent of
\{X_{s}:s\leq t\}
. Translating their notation into ours, their regression function
f_{0}
corresponds to
f^{*}
, their covariate process
\{X_{t}\}
corresponds to
\{x_{i}\}
, and their observations
\{Y_{t}\}
correspond to
\{y_{i}\}
in the temporal model obtained by setting
\gamma_{i}=0
and
m_{i}=1
in (
1
).

There are, however, several important differences. First,
Ma and Safikhani
(
2022
)
impose dependence only on the covariate
process, whereas our framework allows temporal dependence to act on
the full time-indexed process, including the covariates and the
measurement errors; see
Assumption
4
.

Second, Ma and Safikhani
(
2022
)
consider a hierarchical composition
class in which each coordinate function
g_{i,j}
at level
i

depends on at most
t_{i}
of its
d_{i}
inputs. Although they focus on
the regime
t_{i}\ll d_{i}
, the formal definition of their class also
permits
t_{i}=d_{i}
. In particular, taking
q=0
and
t_{0}=d_{0}=d

recovers the
d
-dimensional Hölder class. Their rate avoids the
curse of dimensionality only when the relevant
t_{i}
are sufficiently
small relative to the corresponding
d_{i}
.
The same qualification applies to
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
. Its
definition does not require the component input dimensions
K
to be
small. The rate benefits from compositional structure only when the
relevant values of
K
are sufficiently small relative to the ambient
dimension. Our formulation has the additional flexibility
that the smoothness and input dimension may vary among component
functions within the same level.

At the estimator level, the two approaches differ.
Ma and Safikhani
(
2022
)
consider the sparse neural network class

F(L,p,s,F)
, where
L
denotes the depth,
p
the layer widths,
s

the number of nonzero parameters, and
F
a uniform bound on the
network output. We instead estimate over the fully connected class

\mathcal{F}(L,r)
.

Despite these differences, both approaches yield rates of the same general form. In
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
, the prediction error is of order

\phi_{n}\log^{7}n,\qquad\text{with }\phi_{n}=\max_{i}n^{-\frac{2\beta_{i}^{*}}{2\beta_{i}^{*}+t_{i}}}.

Here
\beta_{i}^{*}
is the effective smoothness associated with level

i
. The quantity
t_{i}
is an upper bound on the number of input
variables on which each component function at that level depends.

.

Equivalently, setting
p_{i}:=\beta_{i}^{*}
and
K_{i}:=t_{i}
, their rate
can be expressed in our notation as

\max_{i}n^{-\frac{2p_{i}}{2p_{i}+K_{i}}}\log^{7}n.

This is directly comparable to our rate
n^{-\frac{2p}{2p+K}}
up to logarithmic factors, with
p
playing the role of smoothness and
K
the role of intrinsic dimension.

In both works, temporal dependence manifests through additional logarithmic factors relative to the independent case. In
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
, the prediction error rate is of order
\phi_{n}\log^{7}n
, compared to
\phi_{n}\log^{3}n
under independence, so dependence effectively contributes an additional
\log^{4}n
factor.

A similar phenomenon appears in our analysis, where the
\beta
-mixing dependence is handled through a blocking and coupling argument. In our case, the final rate involves a logarithmic factor of order
\log^{7.3}(n)
. As detailed above, this exponent arises from the accumulation of several steps in the proof, including metric entropy bounds, localization arguments, truncation, and the blocking construction used to control dependence. Thus, while the precise logarithmic exponents differ, both approaches show that temporal dependence does not alter the algebraic convergence rate, but instead introduces additional logarithmic complexity reflecting the cost of controlling dependence.

Overall, our result complements
Ma and Safikhani (2022)
by showing that comparable neural-network rates remain valid under a different dependence structure and without restricting attention to sparse network architectures.

3.4
Summary of Rates and Dominant Regimes

The preceding results show how the temporal-spatial risk bound simplifies under different structural assumptions. In the full model, the rate contains two components,

\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad n^{-1},

up to logarithmic factors. The first is the main approximation–estimation term, determined by the total number of observations of order
nm
together with the compositional complexity of
f^{*}
. The second appears at the temporal scale
n
, since the spatial random effects are indexed by time and shared across observations within each time point, and because controlling temporal dependence introduces an additional penalty at that scale.

Balancing these two contributions yields the transition regime given by

m\asymp n^{\frac{K}{2p}},

up to polynomial factors in
\log(nm)
. More precisely, if

m\leq n^{K/(2p)}\,\mathrm{poly}(\log(nm)),

then the approximation–estimation term is dominant, and the rate is mainly driven by the total sample size
nm
. Here
\mathrm{poly}(\log(nm))
denotes a polynomial factor in
\log(nm)
; see Table
1
. If instead

m\geq n^{K/(2p)}\,\mathrm{poly}(\log(nm)),

then the
n^{-1}
-scale contribution becomes dominant. At the boundary, both terms are of the same order.

This comparison also clarifies the role of the different model components. Removing temporal dependence leaves the algebraic form of the approximation–estimation term unchanged, but improves the logarithmic factor by eliminating the blocking and coupling cost. Removing the spatial random effects eliminates the
n^{-1}
-scale contribution entirely, leaving only the approximation–estimation term. Table
1
summarizes these special cases and places them alongside the most closely related results in the literature.

Table 1:
Summary of rates, assumptions, and dominant regimes. The regime boundaries are stated up to polynomial factors in
\log(nm)
. That is,
\mathrm{poly}(\log(nm))=(\log(nm))^{c}
with
c=\frac{6.3(2p+K)}{2p}
, arising from balancing the two terms in the rate.

,

-mixing

m\geq n^{K/(2p)}\,\mathrm{poly}(\log(nm))

,  i.i.d.
