4
Theoretical /Results for Manifold Data

Many real-world datasets exhibit low-dimensional structures, such as images as 3D object projections or speech data governed by grammatical rules. Visual, acoustic, and textual data often reflect these structures due to regularities, symmetries, or redundancy, making them well-suited for modeling as samples near low-dimensional manifolds
Tenenbaum et al. (2000)
;
Roweis and Saul (2000)
.

Unlike classical nonparametric estimators, deep neural network estimators have been shown to adapt effectively to low intrinsic-dimensional structures, often mitigating the curse of dimensionality and achieving fast convergence rates. We note that classical methods, such as kernel-based estimators, can also adapt to low-dimensional manifold structures under suitable conditions. However, neural networks offer additional flexibility in capturing complex compositional features of the regression function, which can be advantageous in high-dimensional and structured settings. Studies, including
Cloninger and Klock (2021)
;
Jiao et al. (2023)
;
Kohler et al. (2023)
, have demonstrated their adaptivity to (nearly) manifold inputs.

4.1
Exact Manifold Support

To formalize the low-dimensional structure discussed in Section
4
, we consider the setting in which the covariates lie exactly on a lower-dimensional manifold embedded in the ambient space. This provides a canonical framework to study how the estimation rate depends on the intrinsic dimension rather than the ambient one.

We adopt the notion of a Lipschitz manifold from
Kohler et al. (2023)
.

Definition 4

(Definition 2 of
Kohler et al. (2023)
,

d^{*}
-dimensional Lipschitz-manifold)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be compact set and let
d^{*}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
. Then
\mathcal{M}
is a
d^{*}
-dimensional Lipschitz-manifold if

\mathcal{M}=\bigcup_{l=1}^{R}\mathcal{M}\cap U_{l}=\bigcup_{l=1}^{R}\psi_{l}\left((0,1)^{d^{*}}\right),

where
U_{1},\ldots,U_{R}
is an open covering of
\mathcal{M}
,
where
\psi_{i}:[0,1]^{d^{*}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}(i\in\{1,\ldots,R\})
are bi-Lipschitz functions,
such that
\psi_{l}\left((0,1)^{d^{*}}\right)=\mathcal{M}\cap U_{l}.
Here, bi-Lipschitz means that there exist constants
0<C_{\psi,1}\leq C_{\psi,2}<\infty
such that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} C_{\psi,1}\cdot\left\|a_{1}-a_{2}\right\|\leq\left\|\psi_{l}\left(a_{1}\right)-\psi_{l}\left(a_{2}\right)\right\|\leq C_{\psi,2}\cdot\left\|a_{1}-a_{2}\right\|\end{aligned}

(16)

for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in[0,1]^{d^{*}}
and any
l\in\{1,\ldots,R\}
.

We now incorporate this geometric structure into our temporal–spatial model.

Assumption 5
.

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
, let
y_{ij}=f^{*}(x_{ij})+\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij},

where
x_{ij}
is supported on some
d^{*}
-dimensional Lipschitz-manifold
\mathcal{M}\subseteq[-1,1]^{d}
, and
d^{*}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
. We also assume that the rest of the conditions from Assumptions
1
–
4
hold except for Assumption
3
c
which is replaced with
f^{*}
being

(p,C)
-smooth, where
p=q+s
with
q
and
s
as in Definition
2
. Suppose that for
f^{*}
all its partial derivatives of order less than or equal to
q
are uniformly bounded.

With Assumption
5
in hand, we are now in position to state the main result of this section.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose that
Assumption

5
holds and consider the estimator
\widehat{f}
defined (
2
)
with its number of layers
L
and number of neurons per layer
r
set as either a wide network with
L\asymp\log(nm)
and
r\asymp(nm)^{\frac{d^{*}}{2(2p+d^{*})}}
, or a deep network with

L\asymp\log(nm)\cdot(nm)^{\frac{d^{*}}{2(2p+d^{*})}}
and r = O(1).
Let
\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\left\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\right\}\cdot\log^{1/2}(nm)
and
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
.
Then with probability approaching to one, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}&\lesssim a_{n}\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\right)\left(\frac{\log(nm)}{nm}\right)^{\frac{2p}{2p+d^{*}}}\log^{6.3}(nm)\\
&\quad+a_{n}\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\right)\frac{\log(nm)}{n}.\end{aligned}

(17)

When no spatial noise is present,
\sigma_{\gamma}=0
,
we recover the upper bound obtained in Theorem 1 in
Kohler et al. (2023)
, up to a logarithmic factor.

Theorem
7

is closely related to the exact manifold regime studied in Theorem 6.2 of

Jiao et al. (2023)
. In both settings, the convergence rate is governed by the
intrinsic dimension of the support rather than the ambient dimension, illustrating
the adaptivity of deep neural networks to low-dimensional structure.

To make this comparison precise, we briefly translate their setup into our notation.
In
Jiao et al. (2023)
, the regression model is given by
Y=f_{0}(X)+\eta
, where
the covariates
X
are supported on a compact
d_{M}
-dimensional Riemannian manifold

M\subset[0,1]^{d}
. The target function
f_{0}
is assumed to be
\tilde{p}
-Hölder
smooth on the manifold. In our notation, this corresponds to a regression function

f^{*}
with smoothness level
p
, so that
\tilde{p}
in
Jiao et al. (2023)

plays the same role as
p
in our formulation. After optimizing the network
architecture in Theorem 6.2,
Jiao et al. (2023)
obtain a rate of order

n^{-2\tilde{p}/(2\tilde{p}+d_{M})}
up to logarithmic factors. In comparison,
our result yields a rate consisting of two components: a term of order

(nm)^{-2p/(2p+d^{*})}
, reflecting the intrinsic-dimension dependence of the
estimation problem, and an additional term of order

(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2})\log(nm)/n
, which captures the effect of
temporal dependence and spatial variability. Thus, both results exhibit the same
intrinsic-dimension dependence in the first component of the rate. Despite this similarity, the two results are derived under different assumptions
and apply to distinct statistical frameworks. In
Jiao et al. (2023)
, the analysis
relies on a compact Riemannian manifold structure with additional geometric quantities
such as the condition number (equivalently, reach) and surface area, and is developed
in an i.i.d. regression setting. In contrast, our result is formulated for a
temporal–spatial model under
\beta
-mixing dependence and spatial random effects,
and is based on a Lipschitz-manifold assumption, which does not explicitly impose
curvature or reach conditions. This allows us to extend manifold-adapted rates to
dependent data settings, at the cost of an additional lower-order term of order

(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2})\log(nm)/n
, reflecting temporal dependence
and spatial variability.

The theorem above assumes that the design points lie exactly on a

d^{*}
-dimensional Lipschitz manifold. While this setting is natural, it
can be restrictive in practice, as data may concentrate near a
lower-dimensional manifold without lying exactly on it. Recent work
such as
Jiao et al. (2023)
shows that neural network estimators remain
adaptive when the exact-support requirement is relaxed to approximate
manifold support. It also considers more general low-dimensional sets
characterized by their Minkowski dimension. Motivated by these results,
we extend our temporal–spatial analysis to both settings. The next two
results show that analogous risk bounds continue to hold, with the
intrinsic dimension replaced by an appropriate effective dimension.
Proofs are deferred to Supplementary Material,
Sections S12 and S13.

4.2
Approximate Manifold Support

The exact manifold assumption in the previous subsection can be restrictive in practice, as real data often lie near a low-dimensional structure rather than exactly on it. To account for this, we consider a more flexible setting in which the design points are supported on a neighborhood of a low-dimensional manifold.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset[0,1]^{d}
be a compact
d_{M}
-dimensional Riemannian manifold (in the standard sense; see, e.g.,
Jiao et al. (2023)
), and for
\tau>0
define its
\tau
-neighborhood by

\mathcal{M}_{\tau}:=\left\{x\in[0,1]^{d}:\inf_{y\in\mathcal{M}}\|x-y\|\leq\tau\right\}.

This neighborhood formulation captures the idea that the design points concentrate around a low-dimensional manifold while allowing for small perturbations away from it. The parameter
\tau
controls the scale of this deviation and determines how closely the support adheres to the underlying manifold structure.

The Riemannian manifold used in this setting is related to the
Lipschitz manifold in the exact-support setting. Every compact
Riemannian submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
admits a finite bi-Lipschitz
atlas and therefore satisfies
Definition
4
.
However, a compact Lipschitz manifold need not be
a smooth submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The bi-Lipschitz charts in
that definition are not required to be differentiable.
Thus, the Riemannian framework of

Jiao et al. (2023)
imposes greater regularity on the reference
manifold than the Lipschitz framework of

Kohler et al. (2023)
.

The support condition changes in the opposite direction. Exact support
requires the design points to lie on the reference manifold, whereas
approximate support permits them to lie in its
\tau
-neighborhood.
The displayed definition also gives
\mathcal{M}_{0}=\mathcal{M}
.
Therefore, on the common class of Riemannian manifolds, exact support
corresponds to the zero-radius case. The two settings consequently
trade regularity of the reference manifold against exactness of the
support and are not directly ordered.

These geometric assumptions agree with the approximation results used
in the corresponding proofs. The exact-support proof invokes
Theorem 2 of
Kohler et al. (2023)
, while the approximate-support
proof adapts the construction in the proof of Theorem 6.1 of

Jiao et al. (2023)
.

We now incorporate this geometric condition into the temporal–spatial model.

Assumption 6
.

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
, let

y_{ij}=f^{*}(x_{ij})+\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij},

where
x_{ij}
is supported on
\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
.
We also assume that the remaining conditions from
Assumptions
1
–
4

hold, except that Assumption
3
c
is replaced by

f^{*}
being
(p,C)
-smooth, where
p=q+s
with
q
and
s
as in
Definition
2
. Suppose that all partial derivatives
of
f^{*}
of order less than or equal to
q
are uniformly bounded.

We now state the corresponding risk bound.

Theorem 8

(Approximate manifold support)
.

Suppose that Assumption
6
holds.
Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
d_{\delta}
satisfy

d_{M}\leq d_{\delta}<d,\qquad d_{\delta}\lesssim\frac{d_{M}\log(d/\delta)}{\delta^{2}}.

Assume the approximation-scale condition

\tau^{\,p\wedge 1}\lesssim(nm)^{-\frac{p}{2p+d_{\delta}}}.

Let
\widehat{f}
be the estimator in (
2
), with
(L,r)
chosen according to either

L\asymp\log(nm),\quad r\asymp(nm)^{\frac{d_{\delta}}{2(2p+d_{\delta})}},

or

L\asymp\log(nm)\,(nm)^{\frac{d_{\delta}}{2(2p+d_{\delta})}},\quad r=O(1).

Let

\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\}\sqrt{\log(nm)},
and
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
.
Then, with probability approaching one,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\lesssim\;a_{n}(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1)(nm)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+d_{\delta}}}\log^{7.3}(nm)+a_{n}\frac{(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2})\log(nm)}{n}.\end{aligned}

Theorem
8
shows that the temporal–spatial risk bound continues to hold under approximate manifold support, with the intrinsic dimension replaced by an effective dimension
d_{\delta}
. This reflects the additional geometric complexity introduced by the tubular neighborhood. The lower-order
n^{-1}
-scale contribution remains unchanged, since it is driven by temporal dependence and the time-indexed spatial random effects rather than by the geometry of the support.
In particular, up to logarithmic factors, the first term in the bound matches the convergence rate
n^{-2p/(2p+d_{\delta})}
established in
Jiao et al. (2023)
for the approximate manifold setting. Our result shows that this rate continues to hold in the temporal–spatial framework, with an additional term capturing temporal dependence and spatial variability.
This result is the temporal–spatial analogue of the approximate-manifold regime studied in Theorem 6.1 of
Jiao et al. (2023)
. A proof of Theorem
8
is given in Supplementary Material, Section
S12
.

4.3
Low Minkowski Dimension Support

We now move to a more general regime in which no manifold structure is assumed. Instead, we only require that the support of the design distribution has low geometric complexity, quantified through its Minkowski dimension
Jiao et al. (2023)
. This setting captures a broad class of low-dimensional structures beyond smooth manifolds.

Definition 5

(Minkowski dimension)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be bounded. For
\epsilon>0
, let

\mathcal{N}(\epsilon,\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|)
denote the covering number of
\mathcal{M}
by Euclidean balls of radius
\epsilon
.

The (upper) Minkowski dimension of
\mathcal{M}
is defined as

\dim_{M}(\mathcal{M}):=\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\log\mathcal{N}(\epsilon,\mathcal{M},\|\cdot\|)}{\log(\epsilon^{-1})}.

The Minkowski dimension provides a measure of intrinsic geometric complexity based solely on covering properties, without requiring any smooth parametrization. In contrast to the manifold-based settings considered above, this allows for more general low-dimensional supports.

We now incorporate this geometric condition into the temporal–spatial model.

This condition is also related to, but distinct from, the two preceding
manifold assumptions. A compact
d^{*}
-dimensional Lipschitz manifold
has Minkowski dimension
d^{*}
, see
Falconer (2003)
. Hence, when
d^{*}<d
, exact manifold
support is a special case of the low-Minkowski-dimension setting. By
contrast, for any fixed
\tau>0
, the neighborhood

\mathcal{M}_{\tau}
generally has ambient Minkowski dimension
d
, even
when
\mathcal{M}
is low-dimensional. Approximate manifold support
therefore does not generally imply low Minkowski dimension, see for instance Chapter 3 in
Falconer (2003)
.

Conversely, a set of low Minkowski dimension need not possess either
the bi-Lipschitz parametrizations of the exact-support setting or the
Riemannian structure of the approximate-support setting. The
approximate-manifold and low-Minkowski-dimension assumptions thus
describe different extensions of exact manifold support.

Assumption 7
.

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m_{i}
, let

y_{ij}=f^{*}(x_{ij})+\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij},

where
x_{ij}
is supported on a set
\mathcal{M}\subset[0,1]^{d}
with Minkowski dimension

d^{*}:=\dim_{M}(\mathcal{M})<d.

We also assume that the remaining conditions from
Assumptions
1
–
4

hold, except that Assumption
3
c
is replaced by

f^{*}
being
(p,C)
-smooth, where
p=q+s
with
q
and
s
as in
Definition
2
. Suppose that all partial derivatives
of
f^{*}
of order less than or equal to
q
are uniformly bounded.

Now we state the main result of this section.

Theorem 9

(Low Minkowski dimension support)
.

Suppose that Assumption
7
holds.
Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
d_{0}\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
satisfy

d\geq d_{0}\geq\kappa\frac{d^{*}}{\delta^{2}},

for some constant
\kappa>0
.
Let
\widehat{f}
be defined as in (
2
), and choose
(L,r)
according to either one of the following two regimes:

Case 1.

\displaystyle L\asymp\log(nm),\qquad r\asymp(nm)^{\frac{d_{0}}{2(2p+d_{0})}},

Case 2.

\displaystyle L\asymp\log(nm)\,(nm)^{\frac{d_{0}}{2(2p+d_{0})}},\qquad r=O(1).

Let

\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\}\sqrt{\log(nm)},
and
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
.
Then, with probability approaching one,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\lesssim\;a_{n}(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1)(nm)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+d_{0}}}\log^{7.3}(nm)+a_{n}\frac{(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2})\log(nm)}{n}.\end{aligned}

Theorem
9
extends the temporal–spatial
risk bound to the case where the support of the design distribution is a
general bounded set of low Minkowski dimension. In contrast to the manifold
settings above, no smooth chart structure is required. The effective dimension
in the rate is
d_{0}
, which arises from the random projection argument used
to reduce the ambient dimension. In the corresponding analysis of

Jiao et al. (2023)
,
d_{0}
can be chosen of order
d^{*}/\delta^{2}
, up to
additional logarithmic terms. This is precisely the low-Minkowski-dimension
regime studied in Theorem 6.3 of
Jiao et al. (2023)
. This matches the intrinsic-dimension-dependent rate obtained in

Jiao et al. (2023)
up to logarithmic factors, with
d_{0}
playing the role
of the effective dimension. A proof of
Theorem
9
is given in Section
S13

of the Supplementary Material.

5
Numerical Experiments

5.1
Simulated data

To conduct our simulations, we consider various generative models, referred to as scenarios. We describe these scenarios in the following subsections. The performance of each method is evaluated by the MSE obtained when using the true regression function. We then report the average MSE over 100 Monte Carlo simulations.

For each setting below, we consider dimension
d\in\{2,5,7,10\}.
For each fix dimension,
we vary
n
in
\{500,1000,2000\}
. Moreover, for each fix value
n
we then vary a parameter
m_{mult}
in
\{1,2\}
, to obtain different sets of values for
\{m_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Specifically, we consider: 1).
m_{1}=\ldots=m_{n/4}=16m_{mult}
; 2).
m_{n/4+1}=\ldots=m_{n/2}=24m_{mult}
; 3).
m_{n/2+1}=\ldots=m_{3n/4}=20m_{mult}
; 4).
m_{3n/4+1}=\ldots=m_{n}=10m_{mult}
.

The spatial noise is generated as
\gamma_{i}(x)=0.5\gamma_{i-1}(x)+\sum_{t=1}^{25}t^{-1}b_{t,i}h_{t}(x)
, where

\Bigl\{h_{t}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\sin(t\pi x^{(j)})\Bigr\}_{t=1}^{25},

are basis functions and
\{b_{t,i}\}_{t=1,i=1}^{25,n}
are considered to be i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

The measurement error is generated as follows: For each
i\in[n]
, the vector
\tilde{\epsilon}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
is generated recursively as,
\tilde{\epsilon}_{i}=0.3\tilde{\epsilon}_{i-1}+\xi_{i}
, where
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are independent errors with
\xi_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.5\mathbf{I}_{M})
, and
\mathbf{I}_{M}
is the
M\times M
identity matrix.
To form the measurement errors, for each
i\in[n]
, we take the first
m_{i}
entries of the vector
\tilde{\epsilon}_{i}
and denote the resulting vector as
\epsilon_{i}
, since the
m_{i}
vary across
i
.

We observe the noisy temporal-spatial data
\{y_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
at design points
\{x_{ij}\}_{j=1}^{m_{1}}
sampled in
[0,1]^{d}
as follows.

First, we generate
\{x_{1j}\}_{j=1}^{m_{1}}\sim\text{Unif}([0,1]^{d})
. Then, for any
1<i\leq n
,

x_{ij}=\begin{cases}x_{i-1j},&\text{with probability equals to }0.1,\\
x_{ij}\sim\text{Unif}([0,1]^{d}),&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

for all
j=1,\ldots,m_{i}
.

We investigate the subsequent scenarios.

\bullet

Scenario 1
In this scenario, we use a step function. The function
f^{*}:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

f^{*}(q)=\begin{cases}2&\begin{aligned} \text{ if }\left\|q-q_{1}\right\|_{2}<\min\left\{\left\|q-q_{2}\right\|_{2},\left\|q-q_{3}\right\|_{2},\left\|q-q_{4}\right\|_{2}\right\},\end{aligned}\\[6.00006pt]
1&\begin{aligned} \text{ if }\left\|q-q_{2}\right\|_{2}<\min,\left\{\left\|q-q_{1}\right\|_{2},\left\|q-q_{3}\right\|_{2},\left\|q-q_{4}\right\|_{2}\right\},\end{aligned}\\[6.00006pt]
0&\begin{aligned} \text{ if }\left\|q-q_{3}\right\|_{2}<\min\left\{\left\|q-q_{1}\right\|_{2},\left\|q-q_{2}\right\|_{2},\left\|q-q_{4}\right\|_{2}\right\},\end{aligned}\\[6.00006pt]
-1&\text{ otherwise, }\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle q_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{1}_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T},\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{d-\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T}\right)^{T},

\displaystyle q_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T},\frac{1}{4}\mathbf{1}_{d-\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T}\right)^{T},

\displaystyle q_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{3}{4}\mathbf{1}_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T},\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{d-\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T}\right)^{T}

and

q_{4}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{1}_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T},\frac{3}{4}\mathbf{1}_{d-\lfloor d/2\rfloor}^{T}\right)^{T}.

\bullet

Scenario 2
In this scenario, we apply a simple sine function to the sum of inputs to examine smooth, periodic variation across the input space.
The function
f^{*}:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as
f^{*}(q)=\sin(\mathbf{1}_{d}^{T}q).

\bullet

Scenario 3
This scenario uses a two-level hierarchical function to test the performance on layered non-linearities.
We set

\displaystyle f^{*}(q)

\displaystyle=g_{2}\circ g_{1}(q),\qquad\forall q\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle g_{1}(q)

\displaystyle=\left(\sqrt{q_{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}q_{i}\cdot q_{i+1},\cos\left(2\pi\sum_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}\right)\right)^{\top},\qquad\forall q\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle g_{2}(q)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{q_{1}+q_{2}^{2}}+q_{1}^{2}\cdot q_{2},\qquad\forall q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

\bullet

Scenario 4

This scenario employs a three-level hierarchical function with distinct transformations at each level to test adaptability to deeper compositions with layered structures with varying smoothness.
Specifically, the function
f^{*}\,:\,[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as
f^{*}(q)=g_{3}\circ g_{2}\circ g_{1}(q)
, where

\displaystyle g_{1}(q)

\displaystyle=(\sqrt{q_{1}^{2}+\sum_{j=2}^{d}q_{j}},(\sum_{j=1}^{d}q_{j})^{3})^{\top},\qquad q\in[0,1]^{d},

\displaystyle g_{2}(q)

\displaystyle=(|q_{1}|,q_{2}\cdot q_{1})^{\top},\qquad q\in[0,1]^{2},

\displaystyle g_{3}(q)

\displaystyle=q_{1}+\sqrt{q_{1}+q_{2}},\qquad q\in[0,1]^{2}.

\bullet

Scenario 5
In this scenario, we use a highly non-linear function with interaction terms:

\displaystyle f^{*}(q)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\begin{aligned} \sin(2\pi q_{1}q_{2}),\qquad\text{if }d=2,\quad q\in[0,1]^{d}.\end{aligned}\\[6.00006pt]
\begin{aligned} \sin(2\pi q_{1}q_{2})+\cos(2\pi q_{3}),\qquad\text{if }d=3,\end{aligned}\\[6.00006pt]
\begin{aligned} \sin(2\pi q_{1}q_{2})+\cos(2\pi q_{3}q_{4})+\ldots+\sin(2\pi q_{d-1}q_{d}),\qquad\text{if }d\text{ is even }d\geq 4,\end{aligned}\\[6.00006pt]
\begin{aligned} \sin(2\pi q_{1}q_{2})+\cos(2\pi q_{3}q_{4})+\ldots+\sin(2\pi q_{d}),\qquad\text{if }d\text{ is odd }d>4.\end{aligned}\end{cases}

\bullet

Scenario 6

In this scenario, we use a function with a hierarchical structure that combines quadratic and sinusoidal transformations across separate partitions of the input space.
Here, we use a function with a hierarchical structure:

f^{*}(q)=\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor d/2\rfloor}q_{i}^{2}\Bigr)\cdot\sin\Bigl(\sum_{i=\lfloor d/2\rfloor+1}^{d}q_{i}\Bigr),\quad q\in[0,1]^{d}.

We compare our estimator with four competitors, with their abbreviated names provided in parentheses.

K
-NN-FL
(KNN-FL,
Madrid Padilla et al. (2020)
). We construct the
K
-NN graphs using standard
Matlab
functions such
as
knnsearch
and
bsxfun
.
We solve the fused lasso with the parametric max-flow algorithm from
Chambolle and Darbon (2009)
, for which software is available from the authors’ website,
http://www.cmap.polytechnique.fr/~antonin/software/
. We let the number of neighbors in
K
-NN-FL be
K=5,10,15
, and the parameter
\lambda
to be adjusted with
5
-fold cross-validation.

Additive Model with Trend Filtering
(Trend Filtering,
Sadhanala and Tibshirani (2019)
), a multivariate estimator with additive modes. This is implemented by using the backfitting algorithm to fit each component of the univariate trend filtering estimator. For the univariate trend filtering solver,
we used the
trendfilter
function from the
R
package
glmgen
, which is an
implementation of the fast ADMM algorithm given in
Ramdas and Tibshirani (2016)
. The order of trend filtering (the order of weak derivatives of total variation difference)
k
is chosen from
0,1,2
, and the regularization parameter
\lambda
is chosen by cross-validation.

Generalized Additive Models
(GAM,
Wood (2017)
), implemented in the
Python
package
pyGAM
. The feature functions are built using penalized B splines. The number of splines, the spline order, the type of basis functions, the strength of smoothing penalty
\lambda
are all chosen by cross-validation.
Kernel ridge regression
(RKHS,
Hofmann et al. (2008)
), implemented in the
Python
package
sklearn.kernel_ridge
, with the regularization strength parameter
\alpha
and type of kernels treated as tuning parameters, the best parameters are selected with
5
-fold cross-validation.

For the Dense NN, we employ a fully connected feedforward architecture comprising an input layer,
L
hidden layers, and an output layer. Each hidden layer consists of
r
units and uses the ReLU activation function. The network parameters are updated using the Adam optimizer with a learning rate of
0.001
.
The Adam optimizer is run for a maximum of
800
iterations with early stopping enabled, so that training terminates automatically once the improvement in the loss falls below a prescribed tolerance. This iteration budget is chosen to be sufficiently large to ensure convergence across all scenarios and hyperparameter configurations.
The best parameters
L,r
are selected with
5
-fold cross-validation.

Figure
1
displays the best-performing methods for
Scenarios 1
to
6
with dimensions
d=7
. Figures
2
shows the results for
d=10
. Detailed performance values for each method, an the other values of
d
, along with visualizations for
d=2
, are available in Supplementary Material
S9
.
The evaluation metric we used is the relative error, which is defined as
\text{Relative Error}=\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{nm}^{2}/\|f^{*}\|_{nm}^{2}.

Figure 1:
Comparison of methods across different scenarios,
d=7
,
n
, and
m_{mult}
by box-plot. For each
(n,m_{mult})
setting, we present the errors of the competitors in the range of within 5 times the smallest error.

Figure 2:
Comparison of methods across different scenarios,
d=10
,
n
, and
m_{mult}
by box-plot.

Overall, the Dense NN estimator exhibits a clear decreasing trend in relative error as the sample size increases, and consistently achieves the smallest (or among the smallest) errors across scenarios, although for small sample sizes and moderate increases (e.g., doubling from
n=500
to
n=1000
) the decrease may not be strictly monotonic.

Notably, the Dense NN estimator defined in (
2
) consistently outperforms all other methods.

In each scenario, as spatial noise, measurement error, and mixture probability vary, the Dense NN estimator exhibits an overall decreasing trend in relative error as the sample size increases. Importantly, this trend is observed in most settings, including relatively small increases in sample size (e.g., doubling from
n=500
to
n=1000
), although it is not strictly monotonic in every case. The overall behavior aligns with our theoretical findings, where the Dense NN estimator attains fast convergence rates for hierarchical composition functions under broad structural assumptions.

5.2
Real data application

We consider the EPA Regional dataset, which consists of daily ozone measurements collected across various monitoring stations in different regions of the United States (
https://www.epa.gov/enviro/data-downloads
). The dataset includes measurements of ozone concentration and associated variables such as Air Quality Index (AQI), wind speed, temperature, Latitude, and Longitude. The specific variables in the dataset are: State Code, County Code, Site Number, Latitude, Longitude, Date (Year, Month, Day), Ozone (in parts per million), AQI, Wind (in miles per hour), and Temperature (in Fahrenheit). Data were collected daily over multiple years.

(a) Ozone,
y

(b) Dense NN,
\hat{y}

(c) GAM,
\hat{y}

(d) KNN-FL,
\hat{y}

(e) RKHS,
\hat{y}

(f) Trend Filtering,
\hat{y}

Figure 3:
Visualization of predicted ozone levels for the 45 monitoring locations on March 3rd. Subplots (a) show the
y
, the observed ozone levels, while subplots (b) to (f) represent predictions from Dense NN, GAM, KNN-FL, RKHS, and Trend Filtering, respectively.

For our analysis, we focus on predicting daily ozone levels based on these geographic and meteorological variables. The data consist of monitoring stations across different states in 10 EPA regions, ensuring a representative variety of locations. The observations cover an entire year of daily measurements. The regions considered include latitude and longitude ranges of
63^{\circ}N-70^{\circ}N
and
148^{\circ}W-153^{\circ}W
, respectively, including several representative sites across the continental United States.

To implement our analysis, we have applied our proposed Dense NN method alongside other methods such as GAM, KNN-FL, Trend Filtering, and RKHS. For each model, we used daily data from the year 2023. A total of 260 days were considered for each region, with an average of 40 sites or stations per day, resulting in 12,240 measurements. Categorical variables were encoded using one-hot encoding, and numeric variables were scaled to the range
[0,1]
. We perform 50 random train-test splits, dividing the data into training and test sets using a random
75\%/25\%
split for each EPA region. All models were trained on the training data, and 5-fold cross-validation was used to select tuning parameters. Prediction performance was evaluated on the test set for each method. We assessed the accuracy of the ozone predictions based on relative error defined as:

\text{Relative Error}=\|\hat{f}-\tilde{y}\|_{nm}^{2}/\|\tilde{y}\|_{nm}^{2}
, where
\tilde{y}
is the test set, and with an abuse of notation
\hat{f}
is the vectorized version of the estimator at the locations corresponding to the test data. The final prediction performance is reported as the mean of the test set relative errors and the standard deviation of these errors across the 50 test splits.

Table
2
shows that the Dense NN achieved the lowest relative error among all methods. Figure
3
provides visualizations of the selected regions analyzed in this study. March 3rd was chosen as a representative day from the year-long dataset to streamline the analysis and enhance clarity. The figure’s results further corroborate those presented in Table
2
. Our proposed method, Dense NN, produces predictions that are closest to the true ozone levels.

6
Conclusion

This paper examines fully connected deep neural networks (Dense NN) with ReLU activation for nonparametric regression under temporal and spatial dependencies, demonstrating robust non-asymptotic convergence rates despite such complexities.

Future research could explore relaxing the assumption of exponentially decaying mixing coefficients to polynomial decay or weaker dependencies, broadening the applicability of the results. Additionally, investigating Dense NN with alternative activation functions could enhance versatility, and extending the theoretical framework to accommodate these functions would be a valuable contribution.

Supplementary material for “Dense ReLU Neural Networks for Temporal-Spatial Modeling”

Keywords:

Nonparametric statistics, deep learning, manifold data.

Supplementary Material

S1
Outline of the proof of Theorem 1, Theorem 5 in the Main Manuscript

Theorem 1
:
The proof of Theorem 1 in the Main Manuscript is divided into two critical steps.
The initial step entails a decomposition of the estimation error, measured via the
\mathcal{L}_{2}
norm. In doing this, we use two key features. A minimization attribute and the coupling characteristic between empirical and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
norms. The subsequent step is dedicated to the derivation of deviation bounds. These bounds incorporate Rademacher complexities and pertain to measurement errors as well as spatial noise.

Both stages come with a significant challenge, the interplay between time and space, known as the temporal-spatial dependence. To provide an overview of how we tackled this throughout the proof, in the following we expose our strategy. In Supplementary Material
S3.2.1
, a blocking method is crafted, which effectively transforms dependent
\beta
-mixing sequences into a sequence of independent blocks.
The number of these blocks scales as
O(\log(n))
. By analyzing each block independently, we ensure that the examination of the
\beta
-mixing sequences is confined within these blocks. This approach ultimately allows us to analyze a process of length
O(\log(n))
, leveraging the independence within each block to manage the temporal-spatial dependence.

Proof Sketch:

•

Step
1
:

We find an approximator
\overline{f}\in\mathcal{F}(L,r)
such that

\displaystyle\left\|f^{*}-\overline{f}\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq 2\phi_{nm},

where
\phi_{nm}
is the upper bound of approximation error for class
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
, as shown in (
5
). Choosing
\mathcal{A}_{nm}=\max\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\}\cdot o\left(\log(nm)\right)
, leads to
\left\|\overline{f}\right\|_{\infty}\leq{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
.

•

Step
2
:
Next, we decompose the
\mathcal{L}_{2}
error of estimator as

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq 2\|\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}+2\|\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\phi_{nm}+2\|\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}.

(1)

•

Step
3
:
Our subsequent objective is to transition from the
\mathcal{L}_{2}
norm to the empirical norm.
Lemma

20
provides a coupling of empirical and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
norms. Specificially, with high probability,

\displaystyle\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq 2\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}+C\bigl\{\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(\frac{nm}{S})+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)\bigr\},

(2)

where
S\asymp\log(n)
.

•

Step
4
:
At this point, coupled with the minimization property of
\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{nm}^{2}\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{32}\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{nm}^{2}+16\phi_{nm}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\,\,\,\,+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}\bigl\{\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij}\bigr\}.

•

Step
5
:

To analyze the second term where involving
\epsilon_{ij}
, by
Lemma

25
and
Corollary

19
, with high probability, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\}(x_{ij})\epsilon_{ij}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathcal{A}_{nm}\cdot\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\bigl\{\log^{4.3}\bigl(nm\bigr)\log(\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{nm}^{-1})\bigr\}+\frac{1}{32}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}.

Then by
Lemma

29
,
Corollary

13
,
Lemma

20
, also with high probability, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\}(x_{ij})\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}+\frac{3}{16}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}+C\bigl\{\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)\bigr\}.

Putting the previous steps together, it follows that

\displaystyle\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{n}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}\leq C_{4}\bigl\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{1}{n}\bigr\},

(3)

which, together with (
1
) and (
2
), holds with high probability.

Then, assuming Assumption 1
a
, choosing
\mathcal{A}_{nm}=\max\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\}\cdot o\left(\log(nm)\right)
, and recalling

\displaystyle\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{nm}{S}\right)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+K}},

we obtain the final bound in Equation (
3
) of the Main Manuscript.

Theorem 5
:
For inputs on a low-dimensional manifold, the approximation theory leads to a different result.
.

When the input variable
x_{ij}
is supported on some
d^{*}
-dimensional Lipschitz-manifold
\mathcal{M}\subseteq[0,1]^{d}
, and
d^{*}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, the approximation error is
\sqrt{\phi_{nm,\mathcal{M}}}
, presented in (
42
).

We use
\phi_{nm,\mathcal{M}}
throughout our proof to derive new deviation bounds.

The proof steps for the manifold case follow a similar logical structure to those in the non-manifold case, thus we omit them.

S2
Proof of Theorem 1 in the Main Manuscript

S2.1
Preliminary Result for Proof of Theorem 1 in the Main Manuscript

Lemma 1

(Neural Networks Approximation Result.)
.

Suppose that
f^{*}\in\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
, for some
l\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
and
\mathcal{P}\subseteq[1,\infty)\times\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, where
\mathcal{H}(l,\mathcal{P})
is defined in Definition 3 in the Main Manuscript. In addition, assume that each function
g
in the definition of
f^{*}
can have different smoothness
p_{g}=q_{g}+s_{g}
, for
q_{g}\in\mathbf{N}
,
s_{g}\in(0,1]
, and of potentially different input dimension
K_{g}
, so that
(p_{g},K_{g})\in\mathcal{P}
. Let
K_{\max}
be the largest input dimension and
p_{\max}
the largest smoothness of any of the functions
g
. Suppose that for each
g
all the partial derivatives of order less than or equal to
q_{g}
are uniformly bounded by constant
C_{\mathrm{Smooth}}
, and each function
g
is Lipschitz continuous
with Lipschitz constant
C_{\mathrm{Lip}}\geq 1
. Also, assume that
\max\{p_{\max},K_{\max}\}=O(1)
.

Let

\displaystyle\phi_{nm}=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(nm\right)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+K}},

where
(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}
, with smoothness constraint
p
and order constraint
K
.

Let
L_{nm},r_{nm}\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, then there exists a neural network
f\in\mathcal{F}(L,r)
such that it can either take the form as

\displaystyle\textbf{Case 1.}\quad\text{a wide network with}\quad L=L_{nm}\asymp\log(nm),\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad r=r_{nm}\asymp\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}},

\displaystyle\textbf{Case 2.}\quad\text{a deep network with}\quad L=L_{nm}\asymp\log(nm)\cdot\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}},\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad r=O(1).

(4)

In either case, it holds that

\displaystyle\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}(L,r)}\|f-f^{*}\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\phi_{nm}}.

(5)

Thus, by the choice of
L
and
r
in (
4
), we have

\displaystyle Lr

\displaystyle\asymp\log\left(nm\right)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(nm\right)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}},

\displaystyle Lr^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\log\left(nm\right)\left[\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(nm\right)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}}\right]^{2}\lesssim nm.

(6)

Proof.

The result follows from Theorem 3 of
Kohler and Langer (2021)
.

Under the conditions in (
4
),
then there exists a neural network
\hat{f}_{\text{net}}\in\mathcal{F}(L,r)
such that

\displaystyle\left\|f^{*}-\hat{f}_{\text{net}}\right\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\phi_{nm}}.

Overall, we have

\displaystyle Lr\asymp\log\left(nm\right)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(nm\right)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}},

\displaystyle Lr^{2}\lesssim\log\left(nm\right)\left[\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(nm\right)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}}\right]^{2}\lesssim nm.

∎

Remark 1
.

The rate above indicates that the networks of the class
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
, which is the so-called fully connected feedfoward neural networks class, can achieve the
\ell_{\infty}
-error bound that does not depend on the dimension of predictors
d
and therefore circumvents the curse of dimensionality.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose Assumption 1 holds. Define

\left\|\gamma_{i}\right\|_{\infty,nm}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq m_{i}}\left|\gamma_{i}\left(x_{ij}\right)\right|.

Then there exist parameters
\mathcal{B}_{nm}
and
\mathcal{C}_{nm}
depending only on
n,m
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\epsilon_{ij}|\leq\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{B}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq m_{i}\bigr)=1-o(1),

(7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\gamma_{i}\|_{\infty,nm}\leq\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{C}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n\bigr)=1-o(1),

(8)

where
\mathbb{E}(\epsilon_{ij}^{2})\leq\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}<\infty
, and

\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}=\sup_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}\mathbb{E}\gamma_{i}(x)^{2}<\infty.
Specifically,
\mathcal{C}_{nm}\asymp\sqrt{\log(nm)}
and
\mathcal{B}_{nm}\asymp\sqrt{\log(nm)}
.

Proof.

By Assumption 1,
\{\epsilon_{ij}\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
are sub-Gaussian random variables with sub-Gaussian parameter
\sigma_{\epsilon}
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\epsilon_{ij}|\leq\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{B}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n,\ 1\leq j\leq m_{i}\right)=\,1-\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,n\atop j=1,\ldots,m_{i}}|\epsilon_{ij}|\geq\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{B}_{nm}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,1-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\right)\exp\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\frac{-\mathcal{B}_{nm}^{2}}{32\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,1-\exp\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\frac{-\mathcal{B}_{nm}^{2}}{32\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}}+\log\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\right)\right).

Therefore, it suffices to choose

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{nm}\asymp\sqrt{\log(nm)}.

Next, by Assumption 1
b
, there exist constants
c>0
and
M>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\exp\{c\,\gamma_{i}(x)^{2}\}\bigr]\leq M.

Fix
t>0
. Then, for every
x\in[0,1]^{d}
, Markov’s inequality gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(|\gamma_{i}(x)|>t\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\exp\{c\,\gamma_{i}(x)^{2}\}>\exp\{ct^{2}\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-ct^{2})\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[\exp\{c\,\gamma_{i}(x)^{2}\}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq Me^{-ct^{2}}.

Since this bound is uniform in
x
, conditioning on the design points and applying a union bound yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\|\gamma_{i}\|_{\infty,nm}\leq\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{C}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n\right)=\,1-\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq n\atop 1\leq j\leq m_{i}}|\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})|>\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{C}_{nm}}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,1-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\mathbb{P}\left(|\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})|>\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{C}_{nm}}{4}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\,1-M\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}\right)\exp\left(-c\,\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\frac{\mathcal{C}_{nm}^{2}}{16}\right).

Therefore, it suffices to choose

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{nm}\asymp\sqrt{\log(nm)}.

Combining the two bounds above gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\bigl|\epsilon_{ij}\bigr|\leq\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{B}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n,\,1\leq j\leq m_{i}\bigr)=1-o(1),

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\|\gamma_{i}\|_{\infty,nm}\leq\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{C}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n\bigr)=1-o(1).

This completes the proof.
∎

S2.2
Proof of Theorem 1 in the Main Manuscript

To facilitate reading, we provide the Theorem 1 in the Main Manuscript again.

Theorem

(Theorem 1)
.

Suppose Assumption 1 holds and let
\widehat{f}
be the estimator in
\mathcal{F}(L,r)
defined in Equation (2) in the Main Manuscript,
with its number of layers
L
and number of neurons per layer
r
set as either one of the two cases:

\displaystyle\text{{Case 1.} a wide network with}\quad L\asymp\log(nm)\quad\text{and}\quad r\asymp\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}},

\displaystyle\text{{Case 2.} a deep network with}\quad L

\displaystyle\quad\asymp\log(nm)\cdot\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{K}{2(2p+K)}}\quad\text{and}\quad r=O(1).

(9)

Let
\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\left\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\right\}\cdot\log^{1/2}(nm)
and
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
.
Then with probability approaching to one, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq a_{n}\bigl(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\bigr)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\frac{1}{nm}\bigr)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\log^{7.3}(nm)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\bigl(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\bigr)\log(nm)}{n}.

(10)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\left\{\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{nm},\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\mathcal{C}_{n}\right\}\asymp\max\left\{\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot\sqrt{\log(nm)},\sigma_{\gamma}\cdot\sqrt{\log(n)}\right\}
,

or
\max\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\}\cdot\sqrt{\log(nm)}
. Recall
\phi_{nm}=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\left(nm\right)^{-\frac{2p}{2p+K}}
.

Then, we will show that
with probability approaching to one, it holds that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n},

for sufficiently large
n
, with
S\asymp\log(n)
.
Finally, by Assumption 1 for
\beta
coefficients, let
\beta(S)\lesssim\exp(-C_{\beta}S)
, we obtain the bound.

Let

\overline{f}\in\mathcal{F}(L,r)
be such that

\displaystyle\|f^{*}-\overline{f}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq 2\phi_{nm},

(11)

where the existence of
\overline{f}
is valid by
Lemma

1
.
Since
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
can be chosen appropriately large with respect to
n
while
\phi_{nm}
decays with respect to
n
, assume that
\sqrt{2\phi_{nm}}\leq\frac{3}{4}\mathcal{A}_{nm}
. In addition, assume
1\leq\mathcal{A}_{nm}=\max\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\}\cdot\sqrt{\log(nm)}
where
m
and
m_{i}
satisfy
cm\leq m_{i}\leq Cm
for any
i=1,2,\ldots,n
. Then,

\displaystyle\bigl\|\overline{f}\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq\bigl\|f^{*}\bigr\|_{\infty}+\bigl\|f^{*}-\overline{f}\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{A}_{nm}+\sqrt{2\phi_{nm}}\leq{\mathcal{A}_{nm}},

where the second inequality holds by Assumption 1
g
and (
11
).
It follows that
\overline{f}=\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
.
Since

\displaystyle\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq 2\|\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}+2\|\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq 4\phi_{nm}+2\|\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2},

where the second inequality holds by (
11
),
it suffices to show with probability approaching to one,

\displaystyle\|\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\lesssim{\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}.

(12)

The following steps are made conditioning on
\{x_{ij}\}^{n,m_{i}}_{i=1,j=1}
except for (
16
).

Step 1.

Denote the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\bigl\{|\epsilon_{ij}|\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{A}_{nm}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq m_{i}

\displaystyle\quad\text{and}\quad\bigl\|\gamma_{i}\bigr\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{A}_{nm}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n\bigr\}.

Suppose
the event
\mathcal{E}
holds.
By
Lemma

2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(

\displaystyle\left|\epsilon_{ij}\right|\leq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq m_{i}

\displaystyle\text{and }\left\|\gamma_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{4}\text{ for all }1\leq i\leq n\Big)

\displaystyle=1-o(1),\text{ or }

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)

\displaystyle=o(1).

(13)

Note that under
\mathcal{E}
,

|y_{ij}|\leq\|f^{*}\|_{\infty}+|\epsilon_{ij}|+\|\gamma_{i}\|_{\infty,nm}\leq{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
for any
1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq m_{i}
. Here
\left\|\gamma_{i}\right\|_{\infty,nm}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq m_{i}}\left|\gamma_{i}\left(x_{ij}\right)\right|
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\widehat{f}(x_{ij})\bigr)^{2}.

(14)

Additionally, from the minimization property of
\widehat{f}(\cdot)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\widehat{f}(x_{ij})\bigr)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\overline{f}(x_{ij})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\right)^{2},

where the second identity follows from the fact
\overline{f}=\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
mentioned above.
Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)^{2}.

(15)

Rewriting the left-hand side and expanding the squared term, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left(y_{ij}-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl[\bigl(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)+\bigl(\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)\bigr]^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{2}{nm_{i}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)\bigl(\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left(\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\right)^{2}.

Subtracting
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\right)^{2}
from both sides, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left(\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\,-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}{\frac{2}{nm_{i}}}\bigl(y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)\bigl(\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr)

which implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}\bigl\{y_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{nm}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}\bigl\{f^{*}(x_{ij})+\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}\bigl\{f^{*}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}\bigl\{\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})+\epsilon_{ij}\bigr\}.

Step 2.
Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}\bigl\{f^{*}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}(x_{ij})\bigr\}\bigl\{f^{*}(x_{ij})-\overline{f}(x_{ij})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{nm}\sqrt{2\phi_{nm}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{32}\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{nm}^{2}+16\phi_{nm},

where the first inequality follows from the definition of empirical norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{nm}
and (
11
).

Step 3.
By
Lemma

25
and
Corollary

19
applied with
t=1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left\{\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\}(x_{ij})\epsilon_{ij}\leq\;C_{\epsilon,1}\sigma_{\epsilon}\,\Lambda_{n,S}\!\left(\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right),

with high probability, where

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n,S}\!\bigl(\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr):={}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|}{n}\,\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sqrt{\phi_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\log^{4.3}(nm)\,\log\!\bigl(\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}^{-1}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|}{n}\,\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sqrt{\phi_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\log^{4.3}(nm)\,\log\!\bigl(\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}^{-1}\bigr)}.

Step 4.

Let

\displaystyle g:=\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}.

Since both
\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
and
\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
are truncated at level
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
, we have

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\infty}\leq 2\mathcal{A}_{nm}.

Define

\displaystyle Z_{n}:=\sup_{h\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|\frac{\|h\|_{nm}^{2}-\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}}{\eta_{n}\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}+\tau C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{nm/S}\log^{5.3}(nm/S)+\tau^{2}\beta(S)}\bigr|,

with
\tau=2\mathcal{A}_{nm}
.

By Lemma
20
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(Z_{n})\leq 12.

Let
(a_{n})
be any sequence such that
a_{n}\to\infty
, and define

\displaystyle\eta_{n}:=\frac{1}{8a_{n}},

so that
\eta_{n}\in(0,1/4)
for all sufficiently large
n
, and

\displaystyle a_{n}\eta_{n}=\frac{1}{8}.

Applying Markov’s inequality to
Z_{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Pr(Z_{n}>a_{n})\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(Z_{n})}{a_{n}}\leq\frac{12}{a_{n}}\to 0.

Hence, with probability tending to one,

\displaystyle Z_{n}\leq a_{n}.

Therefore, uniformly over
h\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}-\|h\|_{nm}^{2}\leq a_{n}\Bigl(\eta_{n}\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}+\tau C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{nm/S}\log^{5.3}(nm/S)+\tau^{2}\beta(S)\Bigr).

Since
a_{n}\eta_{n}=1/8
, it follows that

\displaystyle\bigl(1-\frac{1}{8}\bigr)\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq\|h\|_{nm}^{2}+a_{n}\Bigl(\tau C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{nm/S}\log^{5.3}(nm/S)+\tau^{2}\beta(S)\Bigr),

that is,

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{8}{7}\|h\|_{nm}^{2}+\frac{8}{7}a_{n}\Bigl(\tau C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{nm/S}\log^{5.3}(nm/S)+\tau^{2}\beta(S)\Bigr).

Using
\tau=2\mathcal{A}_{nm}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{8}{7}\|h\|_{nm}^{2}+\frac{16}{7}a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{nm/S}\log^{5.3}(nm/S)+\frac{32}{7}a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S).

Since
8/7<2
, this yields

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq 2\|h\|_{nm}^{2}+\frac{16}{7}a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{nm/S}\log^{5.3}(nm/S)+\frac{32}{7}a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S).

Applying this bound with
h=\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
, we obtain that with probability tending to one,

\displaystyle\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}\leq 2\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\,a_{n}\bigl\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}\!\bigl(\frac{nm}{S}\bigr)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)\bigr\},

(16)

where
C_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
is a universal constant satisfying
C_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}>0
; for instance, one may take
C_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}=32/7
.

Observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left[\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right](x_{ij})\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\bigl[\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr](x_{ij})\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})-\langle\gamma_{i},\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\gamma_{i},\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}.

Also,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\langle\gamma_{i},\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}=\bigl\langle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i},\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\leq\frac{8\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}+\frac{1}{32}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}+\frac{1}{16}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}+C_{\gamma,1}a_{n}\bigl\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}\!\bigl(\frac{nm}{S}\bigr)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)\bigr\},

where the second inequality follows from Lemma
29
and the last inequality follows from (
16
), for some universal constant
C_{\gamma,1}
satisfying
C_{\gamma,1}>0
.

In addition,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl\{\big[\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\big](x_{ij})\gamma_{i}(x_{ij})-\langle\gamma_{i},\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\rangle_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{16}\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}^{2}+C_{\gamma,2}\sigma_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{n}\phi_{nm}\log^{5.3}(nm)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{8}\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\|_{nm}^{2}+C_{\gamma,3}a_{n}\bigl\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}\!\bigl(\frac{nm}{S}\bigr)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\gamma,2}\sigma_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{n}\phi_{nm}\log^{5.3}(nm),

where the first inequality follows from
Corollary

13
, and the second inequality follows from (
16
), for some universal constants
C_{\gamma,2},C_{\gamma,3}
satisfying
C_{\gamma,2}>0
and
C_{\gamma,3}>0
.

Step 5.
Putting the previous steps together, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{nm}^{2}\leq\;\frac{1}{32}\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{nm}^{2}+16\phi_{nm}+C_{\epsilon,1}\sigma_{\epsilon}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Lambda_{n,S}\!\left(\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right)}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{8\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}+\frac{1}{16}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}+C_{\gamma,2}\sigma_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{n}\phi_{nm}\log^{5.3}(nm)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{8}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}+C_{\star}a_{n}\bigl\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}\!\bigl(\frac{nm}{S}\bigr)+\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)\bigr\},

where
C_{\star}
is a universal constant satisfying
C_{\star}>0
, and

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n,S}\!\bigl(\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr)={}\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|}{n}\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sqrt{\phi_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\log^{4.3}(nm)\,\log\!\bigl(\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}^{-1}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|}{n}\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\sqrt{\phi_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\log^{4.3}(nm)\,\log\!\bigl(\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}^{-1}\bigr)}.

Since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{32}+\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{8}=\frac{7}{32}<\frac{1}{4},

it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{nm}^{2}\leq\;C_{5}\Bigg\{\sigma_{\epsilon}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Lambda_{n,S}\!\left(\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}\Bigg\},

where
C_{5}
is a universal constant satisfying
C_{5}>0
, and where we have absorbed the lower-order terms
16\phi_{nm}
and
C_{\gamma,2}\sigma_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}_{n}\phi_{nm}\log^{5.3}(nm)
into the right-hand side, using
\mathcal{A}_{nm}\geq 1
.

Equivalently,

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right\|_{nm}^{2}\leq\;C_{6}\Bigg\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Lambda_{n,S}\!\left(\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\right)}}

(17)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}}{n}\Bigg\},

(18)

where
C_{6}
is a universal constant satisfying
C_{6}>0
.

We now handle the first term on the right-hand side of (
17
). Let

\displaystyle g:=\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}.

If
g=0
, there is nothing to prove. Hence, in what follows, assume
g\neq 0
. Then, by (
17
),

\displaystyle\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq C_{6}\bigl\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}\,\Lambda_{n,S}(g)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n}\bigr\}.

(19)

We now bound
\Lambda_{n,S}(g)
. Define

\displaystyle w_{e,s}:=\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|}{n},\qquad w_{o,s}:=\frac{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|}{n},\qquad s=0,\ldots,S-1.

Since

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|=n,

we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}=1.

Moreover, by (
33
), there exists a universal constant
C_{\Lambda,1}
satisfying
C_{\Lambda,1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\phi_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\leq C_{\Lambda,1}\,\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}},\qquad\phi_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\leq C_{\Lambda,1}\,\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}},\qquad s=0,\ldots,S-1.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n,S}(g)\leq\;C_{\Lambda,2}\sqrt{\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)}\Bigg[\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}\,\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\sqrt{\log\!\bigl(\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}^{-1}\bigr)}\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}\,\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\sqrt{\log\!\bigl(\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}^{-1}\bigr)}\Bigg],

where
C_{\Lambda,2}
is a universal constant satisfying
C_{\Lambda,2}>0
.

Since
\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
and
\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
are both truncated at level
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
, we have

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\infty}\leq 2\mathcal{A}_{nm}.

Hence, for every
s
,

\displaystyle\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\leq 2\mathcal{A}_{nm},\qquad\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\leq 2\mathcal{A}_{nm}.

Since
2\mathcal{A}_{nm}\geq 1
, it follows that

\displaystyle\log\!\left(\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}^{-1}\right)\leq\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}}\right),

and similarly,

\displaystyle\log\!\left(\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}^{-1}\right)\leq\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}}\right).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n,S}(g)\leq\;C_{\Lambda,2}\sqrt{\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)}\Bigg[\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}\,\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\sqrt{\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}}\bigr)}\qquad\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}\,\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\sqrt{\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}}\bigr)}\Bigg].

Now define

\displaystyle u_{e,s}:=\frac{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}^{2}}{4\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}},\qquad u_{o,s}:=\frac{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}^{2}}{4\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}.

Then
u_{e,s},u_{o,s}\in[0,1]
, and

\displaystyle\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}=2\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{u_{e,s}},\qquad\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}}\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\log(u_{e,s}^{-1}).

Hence,

\displaystyle\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\sqrt{\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}}\bigr)}=\sqrt{2}\,\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{u_{e,s}\log(u_{e,s}^{-1})},

and similarly for the odd blocks.

Consider the function

\displaystyle\psi(u):=\sqrt{u\log(u^{-1})},\qquad u\in(0,1],

and define
\psi(0):=0
. A direct computation shows that
\psi
is concave on
(0,1)
, hence concave on
[0,1]
by continuity. Therefore, by Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}\sqrt{u_{e,s}\log(u_{e,s}^{-1})}+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}\sqrt{u_{o,s}\log(u_{o,s}^{-1})}\leq\;\psi\!\left(\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}u_{e,s}+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}u_{o,s}\right).

Using

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}u_{e,s}+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}u_{o,s}=\frac{1}{4\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}\bigl(\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}^{2}+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|g\|_{nm}^{2}}{4\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}},

we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{e,s}\,\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}\sqrt{\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{e,s}|m}}\bigr)}+\sum_{s=0}^{S-1}w_{o,s}\,\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}\sqrt{\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{|\mathcal{J}_{o,s}|m}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\leq\;\sqrt{2}\,\mathcal{A}_{nm}\,\psi\!\left(\frac{\|g\|_{nm}^{2}}{4\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\;\sqrt{2}\,\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{\frac{\|g\|_{nm}^{2}}{4\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{4\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{\|g\|_{nm}^{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\;\|g\|_{nm}\sqrt{\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\right)}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{n,S}(g)\leq C_{\Lambda,3}\|g\|_{nm}\sqrt{\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\right)},

where
C_{\Lambda,3}
is a universal constant satisfying
C_{\Lambda,3}>0
.

Substituting this bound into (
19
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq\;C_{7}\mathcal{A}_{nm}\|g\|_{nm}\sqrt{\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{7}\Bigg\{a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n}\Bigg\}

for some universal constant
C_{7}
satisfying
C_{7}>0
.

For any
\eta>0
, Young’s inequality gives

\displaystyle ab\leq\eta a^{2}+\frac{1}{4\eta}b^{2}.

Applying this with

\displaystyle a=\|g\|_{nm},\qquad b=C_{7}\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\bigr)},

and choosing
\eta=\frac{1}{4}
, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{7}\mathcal{A}_{nm}\|g\|_{nm}\sqrt{\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\;

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\|g\|_{nm}^{2}+C_{8}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\right)

for some universal constant
C_{8}
satisfying
C_{8}>0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq\;\frac{1}{4}\|g\|_{nm}^{2}+C_{8}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\right)

\displaystyle+C_{7}\Bigg\{a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n}\Bigg\}.

Since
\mathcal{A}_{nm}\geq 1
, the term

\displaystyle a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)

is bounded by a constant multiple of

\displaystyle a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}C_{\eta_{n}}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm).

Hence, after enlarging constants if necessary,

\displaystyle\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq C_{9}\bigl\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\bigl(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n}\bigr\}

(20)

for some universal constant
C_{9}
satisfying
C_{9}>0
.

We now consider two cases.

If
\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq n^{-1}
, then

\displaystyle\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\leq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n},

since
\mathcal{A}_{nm}\geq 1
. Hence the desired bound follows immediately.

Now suppose that
\|g\|_{nm}^{2}>n^{-1}
. Then
\|g\|_{nm}>n^{-1/2}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\log\!\left(\frac{2\mathcal{A}_{nm}}{\|g\|_{nm}}\right)\leq\log\!\left(2\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{n}\right).

Substituting this into (
20
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq C_{9}\Bigg\{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\bigl(2\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n}\Bigg\}.

Since
\mathcal{A}_{nm}
grows at most polynomially in
nm
, there exists a universal constant
C_{10}
satisfying
C_{10}>0
such that

\displaystyle\log\!\left(2\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{n}\right)\leq C_{10}\log(nm),

and hence

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\!\bigl(2\mathcal{A}_{nm}\sqrt{n}\bigr)\leq C_{11}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)

for some universal constant
C_{11}
satisfying
C_{11}>0
. Therefore, in this case,

\displaystyle\|g\|_{nm}^{2}\leq C_{12}\bigl\{a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n}\bigr\}

for some universal constant
C_{12}
satisfying
C_{12}>0
.

Combining the two cases, we conclude that

\displaystyle\bigl\|\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}-\overline{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}\leq C_{13}\bigl\{a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\phi_{\frac{nm}{S}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}}{n}\bigr\}

(21)

for some universal constant
C_{13}
satisfying
C_{13}>0
.

So the above implies that, up to a constant, the rate is

\displaystyle a_{n}(\mathcal{A}^{2}_{nm}+1)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\frac{S}{nm}\bigr)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\log^{5.3}(nm)+a_{n}\mathcal{A}_{nm}^{2}\beta(S)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}.

Our rate is for the truncated estimator
\widehat{f}_{\mathcal{A}_{nm}}
.

a.
By choosing
\mathcal{A}_{nm}\asymp\max\left\{\sigma_{\epsilon},\sigma_{\gamma}\right\}\cdot\sqrt{\log(nm)}
based on
Lemma

2
;

b.
By Assumption 1, if the mixing rate
\beta(S)\lesssim\exp(-C_{\beta}S)
, then it suffices to choose
S=O(\log(n))
.

a
and
b
imply the rate is

\displaystyle a_{n}\bigl(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\bigr)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\frac{1}{nm}\bigr)^{\frac{2p}{2p+K}}\log^{7.3}(nm)+a_{n}\frac{\bigl(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\bigr)\log(nm)}{n}+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n},

which gives the rate in (
10
). The rate holds by selecting the network architecture in (
9
) to guarantee the approximation error.
∎

Corollary 3

(Polynomial Decay for Mixing Coefficient)
.

Under the setting in Theorem 1 in the Main Manuscript, If
\beta(S)\lesssim S^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha>0
.
We can achieve the rate

\displaystyle a_{n}\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1}{n^{\alpha}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n},

with
S=n
and
m\geq\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}n^{\alpha(\frac{2p+K}{2p})}.

If
m\leq\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}n^{\alpha(\frac{2p+K}{2p})},

we achieve the rate

\displaystyle a_{n}(nm)^{\frac{-2p}{2p+K}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n},

with
S=(nm)^{\frac{2p}{2p+2p\alpha+\alpha K}}
and
\alpha\to\infty
.

Proof.

Case a.
If
\beta(S)\lesssim S^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha>0
, and

\displaystyle m\leq n^{\alpha(\frac{2p+K}{2p})}.

Then
S=\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{2p}{2p+2p\alpha+\alpha K}}\leq n
is a valid choice of
S
and this implies the error bound is

\displaystyle a_{n}\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\right)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{-2p\alpha}{2p+2p\alpha+\alpha K}}\log^{6.3}(nm)

\displaystyle+a_{n}\left(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}\right)\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{-2p\alpha}{2p+2p\alpha+\alpha K}}\log(nm)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}.

When
\alpha\to\infty
, so that the data are independent, it follows that this rate goes to

\displaystyle\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}(nm)^{\frac{-2p}{2p+K}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}.

Case b.
If
\beta(S)\lesssim S^{-\alpha}
and

\displaystyle m\geq\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}n^{\alpha(\frac{2p+K}{2p})}.

Then
S=n
is a valid choice and this implies the error bound is

\displaystyle a_{n}\bigl(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\bigr)\cdot\max_{(p,K)\in\mathcal{P}}m^{\frac{-2p}{2p+K}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+a_{n}\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n^{\alpha}}\log^{2}(nm)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq a_{n}\bigl(\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}\log^{2}(nm)\bigr)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}

\displaystyle\leq a_{n}\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}+\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}+1}{n^{\alpha}}\log^{6.3}(nm)+\frac{\sigma_{\gamma}^{2}}{n}.

∎

S3
Additional Technical Results for Theorem 1

S3.1
Deviation Bounds for Independent Case

The proof below assumes the data are independent, meaning the
\beta
coefficients are all zero. We focus on the independent case here, and address the dependent case later in Section
S3.2
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
and recall

\displaystyle\left\|g\right\|_{nm}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}g^{2}\left(x_{ij}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}g^{2}\left(x_{ij}\right).

Let
m
be the is the harmonic mean of
\left\{m_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
defined as

\displaystyle m=\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{m_{i}}\right)^{-1}.

By the assumption that

\displaystyle cm\leq m_{i}\leq Cm,

it follows that for any
i=1,2,\cdots,n
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Cm}\leq\frac{1}{m_{i}}\leq\frac{1}{cm}.

S3.1.1
Deviation bounds for
\epsilon

In this section, we assume
\epsilon
is a vector of independent random variables. The general case is given in Section
S3.2.2
.
Denote

\displaystyle D_{nm}\left(g\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nm_{i}}}g\left(x_{ij}\right)\epsilon_{ij}.

In this section, the analysis is made conditioning on
\left\{x_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
. We denote the conditional probability conditioning on
\left\{x_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
by
\mathbb{P}_{\cdot\mid\left\{x_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}}
, and we use

\mathbb{P}_{\cdot\mid x}
as its shorthand notation.

Corollary 4
.

Let
g,f\in{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
be two deterministic functions. Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\cdot\mid x}\left(D_{nm}(g)\geq t\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{ct^{2}}{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\left\|g\right\|_{nm}^{2}}\right),

(22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\cdot\mid x}\bigl(D_{nm}(g)-D_{nm}(f)\geq t\bigr)\leq\exp\bigl(-\frac{ct^{2}}{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\bigl\|f-g\bigr\|_{nm}^{2}}\bigr).

(23)

Proof.

Since
\left\{\epsilon_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
are i.i.d. from a sub-Gaussian distribution with sub-Gaussian parameter
\sigma_{\epsilon}
. Conditioning on
\left\{x_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
, the first bound is a direct consequence of classical Hoeffding bound. To see this, notice that for
\left\{g(x_{ij})\epsilon_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
are independent sub-Gaussian variables with sub-Gaussian parameter
|g(x_{ij})|\sigma_{\epsilon}
. This implies that
D_{nm}(g)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nm_{i}}}g\left(x_{ij}\right)\epsilon_{ij}

is sub-Gaussian with sub-Gaussian parameters

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}g^{2}\left(x_{ij}\right)}=\sigma_{\epsilon}\left\|g\right\|_{nm}.

The second bound holds from the linearity of
D_{nm}(g)
with respect to g: for
i=1,\dots,n
,
j=1,\dots,m_{i}
, conditioning on
x_{ij}
,
\left[g\left(x_{ij}\right)-f\left(x_{ij}\right)\right]\epsilon_{ij}
are independent sub-Gaussian variables with sub-Gaussian parameter
\left|\left(g-f\right)\left(x_{ij}\right)\right|\sigma_{\epsilon}
. Then conditioning on
\left\{x_{ij}\right\}_{i=1,j=1}^{n,m_{i}}
,

\displaystyle D_{nm}(g)-D_{nm}(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nm_{i}}}\bigl[g\bigl(x_{ij}\bigr)-f\bigl(x_{ij}\bigr)\bigr]\epsilon_{ij}

is sub-Gaussian with parameter

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\left[(g-f)(x_{ij})\right]^{2}}=\sigma_{\epsilon}\left\|g-f\right\|_{nm}.

The desired inequalities now follow from standard sub-Gaussian tail bounds. In particular,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\cdot\mid x}\bigl(D_{nm}(g)\geq t\bigr)\leq\exp\bigl(-\frac{c_{0}t^{2}}{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}g^{2}(x_{ij})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c_{0}t^{2}}{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\left\|g\right\|_{nm}^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\cdot\mid x}\bigl(D_{nm}(g)-D_{nm}(f)\geq t\bigr)\leq\exp\bigl(-\frac{c_{0}t^{2}}{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m_{i}}\frac{1}{nm_{i}}\bigl[g(x_{ij})-f(x_{ij})\bigr]^{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c_{0}t^{2}}{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\cdot\left\|f-g\right\|_{nm}^{2}}\right).

∎

Next, for
\lambda>0
, we introduce the notation

\displaystyle B_{nm}\left(\lambda\right)=\left\{g:\left\|g\right\|_{nm}\leq\lambda\right\}.

Corollary 5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a function class such that

\displaystyle\log\mathcal{N}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{F},\bigl\|\cdot\bigr\|_{nm}\bigr)\leq C^{\prime}nm\phi_{nm}\bigl(\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\bigl(\delta^{-1}\bigr)\bigr).

(24)

For

\displaystyle t\geq\lambda\sqrt{2C^{\prime}\phi_{nm}nm\left\{\log^{4.3}(nm)\log\left(\lambda^{-1}\right)\right\}}

with
\lambda\leq\frac{1}{4}
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\cdot\mid x}\left(\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}\cap B_{nm}\left(\lambda\right)}D_{nm}(g)\geq\sigma_{\epsilon}\cdot t\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c^{\prime}t^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\right),

for some positive constants
c^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}
.

Proof.

For
l\in\mathbf{Z}^{+}
, let
\mathcal{T}_{l}
be the
2^{-l}\lambda
covering set of
\mathcal{F}\cap B_{nm}\left(\lambda\right)
. Then by setting
\delta=2^{-l}\lambda
in (
24
),

\displaystyle\bigl|\mathcal{T}_{l}\bigr|\leq\exp\bigl(C^{\prime}nm\phi_{nm}\log^{4.3}(nm)\bigl\{l\log(2)+\log(\lambda^{-1})\bigr\}\bigr).

Denote
\psi_{l}(g)\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
be such that
